# P02 execution ledger

Append-only working record of the P02 research execution, copied from the
subagent-driven-development workspace
(`.superpowers/sdd/2026-09-05-p02-research-execution/progress.md`), which git
ignores by design. It is tracked here so the record survives a move to another
machine. Every `Ruling:` line is a decision the controller made on the owner's
behalf; every task line records an engine identity, a hash, or a verdict.
Continue appending to the workspace copy during a session and refresh this file
at each checkpoint.

# SDD ledger — plan: docs/superpowers/plans/2026-09-05-p02-research-execution.md
Started 2026-09-05 (controller: Claude Fable 5.1, session_013Dvsi7FhLemRfUyFSQi87A). Branch docs/p02-execution-plan-review; base at plan commit 6dd514b.
Spec authority: research/RUNBOOK.md, design spec §2/§11/§13, docs/planning/p02/acceptance-schema.md, APPROVAL.md (OWNER-2026-09-05-P02-EXEC).

## Preflight scan
| Pair / task | Produces vs consumes | Finding | Ruling |
|---|---|---|---|
| Task 1 (packaging tool) vs every item task Step 8 | research_package.py build → manifest consumed at publication | Item tasks cannot publish before Task 1 lands; research steps 1–7 do not need it | Ruling: item research (steps 1–7) may run concurrently with Task 1; publication waits — cost if wrong: a re-package after Task 1 changes, minutes |
| Item tasks × shared files (research/CLAUDE.md, docs/port/PARAMETERS.md, COMMONALITY.md) | every publication rewrites them | Concurrent items touch the same files | Ruling: only the runner's publication apply (writer lock) writes them, one item at a time; research artifacts are disjoint per run dir — cost if wrong: a refused publication, retry after the lock clears |
| Item tasks vs SDD "one implementer per task" | roles must be separate fresh contexts (runbook §2, schema) | SDD's single implementer would violate actor independence | Ruling: the spec wins — each role is its own fresh subagent; the controller runs only admission, wrapper/runner commands, packaging and commits; the two runbook audits are the task review (judgment = spec compliance, empirical = execution quality); fix loop = synthesizer revision with NEW auditors — cost if wrong: extra review seats, no evidence loss |
| Parallel items vs SDD "never dispatch implementers in parallel" | disjoint run directories | Conflict only on shared files, already serialized | Ruling: up to three Claude subagents and any number of out-of-process Codex/Doxa jobs may run across items; the pilot R38 runs alone until published (runbook §6) — cost if wrong: wasted parallel work if the pilot changes the recipe |
| Item consumer vs owner (e.g. R24 consumes R38; R69 consumes R01) | owned value published to PARAMETERS.md before the consumer is admitted | queue list enforces | No conflict |
| Task 1 vs runner-guide.md / docs/port/README.md edits | Task 1 documents its tool | Task 86 also edits README status | Sequential, no conflict |
| Run directories in git | manifests, inputs, raws, review, operations are durable evidence | not previously decided | Ruling: commit each run directory with its item; research/runs/.locks/ and any target/ are ignored (.gitignore) — cost if wrong: repository size, reversible |
| Identity of Agent-tool subagents | schema requires actor+model+family | no CLI header for Agent-tool runs | Ruling: record the model ID the subagent quotes from its system prompt together with the CLI probe mapping in probes/; Codex identities come from transcript headers — cost if wrong: an identity record challenged at audit, correctable |
| Doxa cost tracking | ceilings USD 40 (pilot) and USD 300 (bulk) | the wrapper records no usage | Ruling: after each Doxa collection, record the provider metadata (tokens, cost fields when present) in the ledger and the run manifest notes; stop paid submissions when the recorded sum reaches the ceiling — cost if wrong: an over-run bounded by one operation |

Lesson (controller): Perplexity rate-limits concurrent sonar-deep-research submissions; four were in progress when the fifth was refused within 17 seconds. Submit at most three Doxa operations per batch and wait for each batch's Perplexity jobs to finish before the next; leave at least 30 seconds between submissions. Doxa aborts the whole operation on a provider submit failure after earlier providers have been created, so a refused provider always leaves a paid orphan.

Owner direction (2026-09-10T01:3xZ): (1) "Right now, we're out of Fable credits, so we should just continue with Opus till we finish wave 0. We should do reviews with Codex in Fable's place." (2) On scope, verbatim: "Let's make sure we're not overcomplicating each question in the research. We should research the various approaches and the gaps, continue from there... For feedback, ports, and adapters, we shouldn't be writing code... These are supposed to be research documents. Later, we would empirically test the findings. We just record all the research, and then what is the exact finding? We could always go back to the research if the decision didn't hold."
Ruling (staffing): Opus 5 replaces Fable 5.1 in the synthesizer and judgment-auditor seats for the rest of Wave 0; Codex/Terra keeps the empirical seat. The owner's "reviews with Codex" cannot be executed literally: research_validation.validate_topic requires the judgment audit's family to be `anthropic` and the two auditors' families to differ, so Codex in both seats fails publication. Opus satisfies both rules; the distinctness checks are on actor ids, not model names, so a fresh Opus auditor is admissible on items whose raw opus report exists under a different actor id. Cost if wrong: a re-audit by Fable when credits return.
Ruling (empirical scope, correcting a controller drift): the synthesizer role prompt's empirical paragraph told each synthesizer to "create a minimal fixture (a `cargo new` project or the files the recommendation needs)" and execute the recommendation. RUNBOOK.md:186-187 asks only that "configuration, command and version-pin recommendations must execute", and the acceptance schema requires an executed command with argv, cwd, toolchain, log and exit 0 — not a fixture. From now on the empirical check verifies claims (registry and API queries at recorded endpoints, resolving a query suite, running the recommended tool against a few sample inputs, reading pinned source) and never implements the recommendation (no working seam, service, hook pipeline or acceptance script). Unproven claims are recorded as unproven in `## Engines` and left for P03 to prove in the real template. Why: the expansion was the controller's, not the owner's; it turned R46 into five rounds of shell-script review and produced the R01 rejection, which faults the throwaway fixture's wiring rather than the research. What it cost to have it: four recommendations changed by executed checks (R41's `cargo metadata --locked` false pass, R06's live clippy lint, R15's broken binary name, R38's header-length semantics), which the narrowed rule still catches because each was a verification, not an implementation. Cost if wrong: decisions carry more unproven claims into P03, where CI is the arbiter. No change to RUNBOOK.md, the acceptance schema or the validator.

Revision 6 dispatched on Opus: correct the stale sentence and apply the owner's 2026-09-10 scope ruling (keep the three findings, demote the hardened script and 436-case harness to an illustrative recommendation with the edge cases P03 must handle, report only verifications in ## Empirical check).
Owner decision (2026-09-10, R01 keystone review), verbatim: "R01 yes it should be accepted as a proposal that then needs to be validate in the implementation phase." Effect: R01's recommendation (port/adapter split; dyn-compatible trait port; core generic over it; production adapter erased to a trait object at a single composition root and handed to both the CLI and the web layer; shipped public in-memory fake; one substitutability suite per adapter; shared serde contract behind both front ends; no DI container, no mocking crate as the primary substitution mechanism, no architecture framework; async-trait conditional on R05) is ACCEPTED as a proposal. Validation happens in P03 against the real template. The 38 items in R01's subtree may be researched against it. R01 still completes its own publication path (revision 2, fresh audit pair, packaging) before dependents are admitted, because the runner gates prerequisites on `resolved` status.
Ruling (programme-wide, from the same owner statement): every P02 decision is a PROPOSAL validated in the implementation phase, not a claim that the design is already proven. This is the general form of the 2026-09-10 empirical-scope ruling: research records approaches, gaps, findings and the exact recommendation, verifying what can be verified cheaply; P03 builds the template and its CI proves the decisions; a decision that does not hold under implementation is revised against the recorded research rather than re-researched from scratch. Decisions state this status explicitly and list what P03 must validate. Cost if wrong: none identified; it matches RUNBOOK.md's own empirical wording and the append-only revision rule already in the spec.

Task (R67, keystone): draft DECISION.md by synth-opus-2026-09-05T155124Z-2271e140a7ef (claude-opus-5), sha256 9722512b5038e308b3d2363db66b9d76e85eab9668e5157008366e3a8ff267e2 — owns error-taxonomy-exit-codes = error-contract/v1: thiserror 2.0.20 in the library, anyhow >=1.0.103 in binaries only, signal-hook 0.4.4 default-features=false on cfg(unix) for the CLI, plus std; an append-only RSLB### catalog from an explicit `const fn as_str()` on a #[non_exhaustive] enum with ALL and a coverage test, never strum or variant-name derivation; exits 0/1/2/3/4/5/130/143 per the org CLI Design Standard R6.1; a separate optional hint field; benign result-stream BrokenPipe exits 0 silently with println! lint-denied; precedence signal → broken pipe → domain → unexpected via main() -> ExitCode; a sanitized 0600 crash sink that never changes the exit code, its path deferred to R57 and a no-op until R57 answers.
Fourteen verifications, all exit 0, logs in review/evidence/: crates.io and GitHub figures for 12 crates; RustSec (a 404 at rustsec.org/packages/<name>.html is the zero-advisory proof, and RUSTSEC-2026-0190 is confirmed unsound and patched at >=1.0.103); published-tarball source reads settling ctrlc (set_handler takes no signal argument and termination fans SIGINT/SIGTERM/SIGHUP into one callback, so it cannot produce 130 vs 143) against signal-hook's per-signal flag::register; std read at the 1.96.0 floor (ExitCode/Termination/From<u8> stable since 1.61.0, print_to panics, the runtime sets SIGPIPE to SIG_IGN, capture is env-gated and force_capture is not); both SIGPIPE tracking issues open plus Cargo's SemVer enum rule; the CLI standard's R6.1/R7.7/R7.8/R8.2/R9.6 read from source; sysexits.h; seven adopter manifests and ripgrep's EPIPE boundary; and E13–E14, which closed a gap both raws left open — manifest-only resolution over three Cargo.toml files (no Rust source written) giving 22 packages, zero duplicates, a maximum dependency rust-version of 1.87.0 and a clean cargo audit, with an anyhow "=1.0.102" control that trips RUSTSEC-2026-0190 and so proves the floor is load-bearing.
BASELINE-REVIEW F301 carried verbatim and NOT adopted, since `- owns:` lists only error-taxonomy-exit-codes: the backtrace rule is stated as an assumption and the boundary recorded, and OpenAI's force_capture proposal is declined — this answers the handoff's open owner question 9. Perplexity's CONFLICT: R57 state-directory is quoted in ## Parameters and recorded without escalation (consumes is empty, R57 registers no parameter, and the no-default-path design removes the dependency). Doxa's BASELINE-REVIEW lines on F295–F300/F302 are noted as miscategorised, those being R67's own DIVERGENT rows, as is Gemini's claim to own five unregistered parameters. Eight engine disagreements resolved with the settling evidence named, plus one synthesis no engine made: R8.2 splits configuration failures the engines lumped together, an unparsable or invalid document exiting 2 and an unreadable layer exiting 1, which gives R54–R56 both entries. No runtime behaviour was executed on any platform; opus's fixture measurements are cited as opus-measured rather than re-run. Eight items recorded as unproven for P03.

Four non-blocking notes for a later revision: the three reference projects cited for design E are all D-shaped by the decision's own citations; "All five engines agree on this framing" overstates, since three engines label the level architectural pattern and OpenAI puts F002/F011 at must-agree levels; the prompt-required issue-responsiveness figure for serde is dropped although raw/opus.md:991-996 captured it (0 of 10 answered) and the omitted figure is the adverse one; and DECISION.md:180 "Nothing else that compiled is relied on" contradicts :109, which closes serde's macOS gap using that same compile. Task (R46): revision 6 by synth-opus-2026-09-05T160304Z-5cba7fe96ae4-r6 (claude-opus-5), sha256 a95cbbfae000d539bb57aaf3d67b71510c22c62457f6e215a219d9640a6808bd — rewritten as research after five rejections that were all defects in an acceptance pipeline P02 was never meant to ship. Finding: Rust imposes no per-file licence header, and the surveyed maintained projects (Serde, Tokio, Axum, the OpenZeppelin template) carry the licence only in Cargo metadata plus root files, while clap's dual manifest against its MIT-only file header is exactly the drift the per-file form invites. Recommendation: no per-file header; declare license = "MIT OR Apache-2.0" once in the manifest alongside F206's root LICENSE-APACHE/LICENSE-MIT pair; no insertion tooling; P03 builds the negative check; the REUSE two-line header is the runner-up if mixed-origin source ever appears. Removed from the decision text: the awk checker, the acceptance sequence, the scan-set code, jq as an R46 dependency and the per-revision hash inventories. ## Empirical check cites the existing r5 logs (header-free workspace builds, tests and packages on five OS and toolchain legs; the root archive shipping both texts while a member archive ships neither; CARGO_PKG_LICENSE; //! placement; the GNU/BSD find loop divergence), the r2/r3 audit logs for the divergent-member and wrong-root-text admissions, the r5 audit log for the target-descendant symlink, and raw/evidence-terra-r4.md for standards syntax; the only commands this revision ran were rustc and cargo --version. Nine items unproven for P03. The untracked review/empirical/acceptance-v6.sh and checker-v6.sh left by the dead Fable agent under the old brief are untouched on disk and explicitly disclaimed in ## Engines; they are never committed and will be removed after publication.

Resumability validation (2026-09-10, owner request): all three Doxa providers were tested for cold retrieval by recorded identifier alone, from a fresh process, against the oldest jobs in the record (the R38 pilot, submitted 2026-09-08T22:38 local, about 27 hours earlier). OpenAI GET /v1/responses/{id}: status completed, store true, expires_at null, background true, 44,533 characters of answer recovered. Perplexity GET /async/chat/completions/{id}: status COMPLETED, 62,548 characters recovered. Gemini interactions.get(id): status completed, 22,745 characters recovered. Doxa-level `status <op>` on the same operation from a cold process reports completed for all three providers with their output paths, and creates nothing. Conclusion: background research is resumable by identifier at both the provider layer and the Doxa layer, so a submitting process may exit or die without losing work.

The controller recorded the auditor's actual executed verification instead, `shasum -a 256 review/DECISION.md`, which is what its log shows it ran to reproduce the decision and history hashes. Task (R67): revision 3 by the resumed synth-opus (claude-opus-5), sha256 37d610925710e4f667c8fbca1f590de6d46ffdc11b8872c2cd1b508b942e2284 — both judgment findings answered. Neither rule covers it, and R6.1's own default for an unlisted failure is 1", with its reasoning and its cost if wrong; the requested sweep for the same defect found a second instance, RSLB204, now carrying the same treatment. The agent stalled on a watchdog after writing the file; the revision was recovered from disk with nothing lost. Fresh auditors r3 to follow.
Tooling (2026-09-10, owner question "where and how is the research captured and rescanned if the session is stopped or restarted"): added scripts/research_scan.py, a read-only scanner that reconstructs the whole programme's state from the repository. It reads research/CLAUDE.md for published status, research/EXECUTION.json for tier and required engines, and every research/runs/<ITEM>/<RUN>/manifest.json with its operations/ and review/ contents, then prints per item the decision revision and hash, the audit verdicts, how many audit rounds have been archived, and the next action; `--todo` filters to items needing action and `--json` gives the machine form. It also checks each audit's recorded decision-sha256 against the decision on disk and reports an audit that no longer binds the current text, which is the defect that would otherwise surface at packaging. Unfinished engine operations, including partial and failed ones, are listed with their operation id and provider job ids so a returning session can collect them by identifier alone. First run reported: done 10, not admitted 52, research 17, synthesize 3, empirical audit 2, collect in-flight 1 (R66's partial Doxa operation). AGENTS.md now tells any future session to start there rather than trusting an agent's memory.
Verification (2026-09-10, owner question "does each directory contain the identifier to pull the finished Doxa reports"): yes, for all nine Doxa runs, and every identifier file is tracked in git. Each run carries the Doxa operation id in manifest.json and operations/doxa.json, and the per-provider job ids in a status or checkpoint file alongside. The check exposed two blind spots in the new scanner rather than in the data: R38, whose Doxa attempt was its third, records them in doxa-status3-initial.json rather than doxa-status-initial.json, and R66, whose operation failed, records them in doxa-checkpoint-at-failure.json where the providers sit at the top level instead of under data. The scanner hardcoded one filename and one shape, so it would have reported no recoverable identifier for exactly the two runs most likely to need recovery: a retried attempt and a failed operation. It now globs every doxa-*.json in the operations directory and merges both shapes, verified against all three cases.

Lesson (controller), third instance of the same family: a correction applied to some occurrences of a claim leaves the document self-contradictory, and the next audit rejects it. R41 lost three rounds to this, R16 one, and now R67 one. A fix brief must require the synthesizer to enumerate every place the claim appears — narrative, table, owned parameter, engines section — and to list those locations in its reply, rather than fixing the lines the auditor happened to cite.

Task (R42): judgment audit r1 by audit-fable-2026-09-05T155503Z-4141a35b69e0 (claude-opus-5) — verdict reject, three findings, audit sha256 f11c4943ba9dffc4fae20820ff81abf175430111cfd3bcbf9f28bca4d4e9c59a, decision hash verified. Finding 1 is a defect in the owned parameter itself, not its wording: the presence test `command -v cargo-<sub>` is unsound for the rustup-component class the parameter explicitly names, because ~/.cargo/bin/cargo-fmt is a symlink to rustup and resolves whether or not the component is installed, so the test cannot fail; the auditor verified that toolchain 1.97.1's bin directory lacks cargo-fmt while `rustup which` for it errors. The controller reproduced this independently: `ls -l ~/.cargo/bin/cargo-fmt` shows a symlink to rustup, `command -v cargo-fmt` succeeds, and `rustup which --toolchain 1.97.1 cargo-fmt` reports the component is not installed. The F181 BASELINE-REVIEW line's claim that this is "provably the correct test for both tool classes" therefore exceeds the stub experiment, which covered crates.io-installed plugins only; the fix is `rustup component list --installed`, which the decision's own check 4 already ran. Finding 2: ## Engines records five disagreements and omits this one, although two Doxa providers proposed exactly that fix, one asking for declarative state rather than an ambient PATH search and the other proposing to eliminate the loop; rejecting the version manager disposes of that tool's half but not of the tool-independent half. Finding 3: the parameter is silent on Cargo aliases, a third invocation form present in two of the four reference justfiles it cites as proving the rule, so a downstream item would have to guess. Explicitly not findings, recorded to avoid re-litigation: the 17-repository survey is scoped honestly and corroborated by an independent structural argument; the licence question is correctly an owner ruling and posed decidably; both BASELINE-REVIEW lines are in runbook form and responsive; the ten unproven items are honest; and building nothing is correct under the research-only ruling. Fix round 1/5.

Task (R18): judgment audit r2 by audit-fable-2026-09-06T043454Z-6f31d86e5b7d-r2 (claude-opus-5) — verdict REJECT, five unresolved findings, audit sha256 4185f6a687e39f3fe25afc08926aa73ce1e270c51789d0353017f0dafc8b1c38, decision hash verified. Asked whether it would ship this to strangers, it answered not as it stands, and gave a precise reason: not the inert-by-default design, whose honesty it endorses, but that the one surface where the enable flag structurally cannot help is the public origin repository, and the security case for that surface rests on a single endpoint returning one row. JA-01: an upstream issue titled "Default workflow template missing author trust gate — secrets exposed on public repos" is reduced to a comment count, and the origin repository's open mention surface, where the enable flag is by construction true, is neither adjudicated nor covered by any unproven item. JA-02: a Doxa provider was dismissed on a collateral version error, but its cited source, which the auditor fetched at HTTP 200, is a distinct CVSS 7.8 chain with variants exploited in the wild, targeting a tag-mode workflow character-identical to the candidate, and carrying no GitHub advisory — so the decision's "exactly one published advisory" is not the security history; the recommended configuration is not the vulnerable pair, so the defect is evidence handling rather than exploitability. JA-03: five engines raised BASELINE-REVIEW lines and the decision carries two while stating that one engine was the only source. JA-04: a claim attributing a three-level ordering to docs/port/README.md, which contains no such list, while the spec says the opposite. JA-05: a draft double-fire from two events with no draft guard, a review-submitted trigger, issue-title matching and a missing fork clause on the assistant job, all objected to by another provider and none adjudicated.
Controller check of JA-03 across the whole programme, because a dropped baseline challenge would be serious: six BASELINE-REVIEW lines exist across four of R18's five reports, confirming the finding. Widening the check to every run that has a decision, no ledger row raised by any report is missing from its decision — every identifier is addressed, including in all published items, so nothing was silently dropped and no published item carries an unadjudicated challenge. What varies is form: some decisions carry the runbook's `BASELINE-REVIEW: F### — principle — proposed change — evidence` line and others address the row in prose. R18's actual defect is narrower than a dropped challenge and still real: it carries two of six lines and misstates which engines raised them, which is a factual error about its own inputs.

The presence test now keys on acquisition rather than name shape: rustup components are verified through the toolchain's own component state, and crates.io or release tools through a path lookup. It was proven with a matrix, running the same nine commands in three directories pinning 1.97.1 with neither component, stable with both, and 1.96.0 with one, and with PATH restricted to the rustup proxies: the path lookup for both component names exits 0 in all three because they are symlinks to rustup, while the component-state test matches the truth in all six cells. The requested sweep covered fifteen invocation and detection claims and found four more defects beyond the audit's: the same unsound test applied to bare proxy names, thirteen of which are listed; built-in subcommands unmentioned, where the path lookup also fails; and aliases unmentioned. It also newly proved that the formatter invocation routes through the proxy and follows the committed channel, producing three distinct builds and failing under a toolchain lacking the component, which narrows an unproven item and shows revision 1's recorded version was Homebrew's rather than the toolchain's. It corrected the auditor's own guess that a recipe in one reference repository used an alias: it is a separate tool's binary, installed by that repository's own recipe. ## Engines gains the omitted disagreement with both providers' lines quoted, recording that one provider's rationale falls with the version-manager rejection while the other's component-state half survives and is the adopted fix. Fresh auditors r2 dispatched.
Lesson (controller, second shell-quoting failure): a Codex audit dispatch failed with a zsh parse error because the prompt text contained a backticked placeholder holding angle brackets, which the shell tried to run as a command substitution containing a redirect. The earlier heredoc failure had the same cause. Rule: never put backticks in a shell-passed prompt string, and never put angle-bracket placeholders inside them; write the identifier in plain words instead. The failure was harmless because the script writes its receipt only on completion, so no audit file was produced and nothing was recorded as done.

DECISION.md:278 says a Cargo alias "silently shadows" a same-named external subcommand, but both the decision's own check and the auditor's rerun show Cargo printing an explicit warning naming the alias, the shadowed executable's path and the upstream issue URL. The corrected sentence now says an alias takes precedence over and shadows a same-named external subcommand, which Cargo reports rather than passing over in silence, quoting the recorded warning including its note that the behaviour will become a hard error; the argument is restated accordingly, since a warned and future-fatal collision is a materially weaker hazard than a silent one, so shadowing drops to the third and lesser reason for defining no alias.
The requested sweep covered eighteen absolute characterisations and corrected ten. Task (R18): revision 3 by the resumed synth-opus (claude-opus-5), sha256 05b41f871b1111781d5ddb97f71bb51bc2091deb8b6454ee964d88cefe3d7c2e. JA-01, the origin repository's open mention surface: the upstream issue was read in full (six months open, zero comments) and its three exposures adjudicated against a six-layer guard table read in source at the pinned version. The decisive finding corrects one of the research reports: federation answers only one of the three exposures, removing the static secret but not the OIDC exposure, which is exactly what the disclosure exfiltrates, so the opus report's claim that federation mitigates the issue is withdrawn. The issue's own author-association prefilter is adopted as a guard clause, being the only control that acts before the job starts.
JA-02, the security history: two entries, not one. Besides the medium advisory patched in 1.0.74 there is the chain the dismissed provider had cited, CVSS 7.8, variants exploited in the wild, fixed as of 1.0.94, carrying no GitHub advisory. That provider's version figure is corroborated by its own primary source; revision 2's refutation had compared it against a different advisory and one release's changelog, a category error now withdrawn, though its proposed pin stays rejected on currency rather than safety. The recommendation is placed outside both attack phases with all four remediations present at the pin.
The recommendation is unchanged, ship both, but now argued rather than asserted, and the runner-up gains a third winning condition: the owner declining the residual prompt-injection exposure of a write-capable lane reachable from trusted-author text that quotes untrusted content. JA-05: the draft double-fire is adopted as a guard with an exactly-once composition; two triggers are kept with their widening stated; and the missing fork clause turned out to be the contract's error rather than the fixture's, since no assistant event is a pull-request event, so the clause now binds the review lane only. JA-03: all seven baseline lines are carried in the required form with disposition and routing, matching the controller's own count. JA-04: the misattributed ranking is withdrawn and cited to the spec's unordered table. Five new unproven items recorded, including that the prefilter actually stops the job starting and that nothing untrusted is reached before the actor checks.
OWNER DECISION NEEDED (R18, now sharper): the recommendation ships two AI workflows whose write-capable lane remains reachable from trusted-author text quoting untrusted content. That residual prompt-injection exposure is argued and bounded rather than eliminated, and the runner-up, shipping neither with an opt-in recipe, wins if the owner declines to accept it. This is a policy call about what a public template may hand to strangers, not a research question.

BOTH AUDITORS NOW SAY DO NOT SHIP. The empirical auditor's assessment, verbatim: "No: the current evidence does not support shipping this public, write-capable workflow bundle to strangers. It supports a conditional P03 proposal after F1–F3 are corrected and the stated P03 runtime checks establish the prefilter and execution ordering. The unpinned marketplace fetch and the acknowledged trusted-author prompt-injection residual remain owner-risk reasons to choose the documented [runner-up]." It confirms the substance of revision 3's security work: at the pinned tag the write-permission check does return true for any bot login, the human-actor check rejects non-user actors by default and both modes call it, federation removes the stored credential but not the identity-token credentials the disclosure describes, the 7.8 rating and in-the-wild exploitation hold, the association prefilter is syntactically valid and is the only pre-job actor-trust guard in the enabled origin case, and four of the five unproven items are honest. It also raises a new concern the decision does not address: an unpinned marketplace fetch. Two independent auditors in different model families have now reached the same conclusion against the recommendation. Fix round 3/5.

Task (R18): revision 4 by the resumed synth-opus (claude-opus-5), sha256 83bf78bae1813ad43a0fb23b150db8d9200281c1f41ed9a5235071fadf25d9f9. THE RECOMMENDATION MOVED, and not simply to the runner-up: ship both workflow files as inert artifacts, but withhold enablement on any public repository, the origin included, until the owner records acceptance of a named residual, with the runner-up as what ships if acceptance is withheld. Its reasoning: shipping files and enabling the capability are separate acts with separate risk owners; the residual is unremovable by configuration, binds both lanes on the input side, and sits behind a pre-token window whose only control is unproven; and since no owner acceptance exists, an unconditional recommendation to ship and enable would assert a judgement this research does not hold. It explicitly declines to claim the runner-up wins outright, noting that shipping neither relocates the decision rather than removing the risk.
Three findings corrected with new facts found in the process. F1: the write-permission check is called once in a shared entrypoint, not per mode, and while correcting it the synthesizer found that the token setup immediately before it mints a GitHub App token requesting write access to contents, pull requests and issues BEFORE either actor check runs; that is now a guard-table row and part of what P03 must prove. F2: the subprocess scrub is recorded as available at the tag but inactive, because the action emits it and installs its isolation dependencies only when a list the recommendation leaves empty is populated; activating it by environment alone would set the flag without its dependencies, recorded as a new unproven item rather than recommended, and the fitness gate now says three of four remediations are active rather than four. F3 scoped to the origin-enabled case. The unpinned marketplace fetch was verified structurally unpinnable, since the URL pattern anchors to the repository suffix, and is cloned from the default branch inside the credentialed job with the marketplace name itself remote content; it is REMOVED from the recommendation and replaced by the maintainer's pinned local form, with only the pin-form evidence routed onward. Three further unproven items added, including that the review lane's token scope is server-chosen and unreadable, so unknown rather than read-only. The synthesizer also disclosed that one of its own evidence blocks recorded a command line that was not what ran, replaced it, and reran the fetch with the real arguments.
OWNER DECISION, now precisely posed (R18): the decision ships the two workflow files but withholds enabling them anywhere public, including this repository, until you record acceptance of a specific residual: a prompt-injection exposure on a write-capable lane, reachable from trusted-author text quoting untrusted content, which no configuration removes and which sits behind a window where an App token with write scopes is minted before any actor check runs. Accept it and the workflows may be enabled; withhold acceptance and the template ships them inert with a documented opt-in recipe. This is now a decision about what you are willing to own, stated in the terms you would need to own it.
Defect found at packaging (R42, and shared by R18 and R25): publication was refused with "assumed msrv-policy does not match current registry value" and the same for the other three fixed parameters, although the values are identical. Cause: the validator reads everything after "= " on an `- assumes` line as the value and compares it byte-for-byte with docs/port/PARAMETERS.md, while these decisions wrote the value in backticks followed by a citation and commentary on the same line. The published decisions written earlier carry the value bare, which is why this never surfaced before. All three pending decisions have it, so it is a contract defect rather than three mistakes: the synthesizer role said the value must be "quoted exactly from docs/port/PARAMETERS.md", which every writer read as an instruction to add backticks. The role contract now states the format explicitly, that the value goes bare with no backticks, citation or commentary on the line, and that anything to say about the parameter belongs in prose after the list.
Lesson (controller): run `research_package.py build` as a cheap pre-check as soon as a decision is written, before dispatching either audit. It validates the acceptance bundle, including parameter formatting, audit binding hashes and evidence paths, and it caught a blocking defect that both audits had passed over. Doing it early would have saved a round on each of these three items.
Task (R42): revision 6 by the resumed synth-opus (claude-opus-5), sha256 ca5a731081e51ab5d0eea91dd9f4ef8a456855df026354ed1f8146bea77f6d7f. The four fixed-parameter lines now carry the registry value bare, verified by parsing both the decision and the registry and comparing as strings, all four byte-identical. It also found a second instance the controller had missed: the owned-parameter line carried a multi-paragraph argument with bold, backticks and check citations, which is the text the registry itself would have received; it is now one bare statement of the rule. Everything those five lines used to carry is preserved in three new paragraphs after the list, with twenty-five substantive fragments of the old text checked as present, including the two load-bearing notes that Windows support is noted where it exists and not required, and that the licence gate's owner ruling is unproven item 7.
Consequence, and the reason the pre-check matters: any edit to a decision unbinds its audits, so this format-only fix invalidated both of R42's approvals and costs a fresh audit pair. The packaging build cannot serve as an early pre-check because it requires review/identities.json, which only exists after both audits complete. So the check moved into scripts/research_scan.py instead: it now parses each unpublished decision's assumes lines the way the validator does, compares them with the registry, and reports "fix parameter lines before auditing" with the offending parameters named. Run against the tree it immediately flagged R18 and R25 with all four parameters each, which would otherwise have failed at publication after their audits, costing each of them a round too.

Task (R25): empirical audit r3 by audit-codex-2026-09-05T164357Z-eec460755121-r3 (gpt-5.6-terra) on revision 3 (sha256 23d42766…) — verdict reject, one finding, audit sha256 b60e21bd68488139340cb7749d225786b42854b156c9b4d72cc2e0fffb44a2ec, decision hash verified. The seeded-manifest consequence is overstated in three places: the decision says seeding the manifest with 0.1.0 makes the first release or release pull request 0.1.1, when the documentation supports only the narrower premise that a manifest entry is the package's last released version, which the tool backfills and then applies the versioning strategy to the subsequent commits. The decision must say the seed makes 0.1.0 the previous release and that the next proposed version depends on the commits that follow, describing 0.1.1 only for the demonstrated TypeScript history or for a stated patch-or-feature-only history. The same audit confirms the revision-3 repair: "the vocabulary and division of labour are now accurate", the tool maintaining release pull requests, the repository merging one, and the tool then tagging the commit. Task (R20): empirical audit r1 by audit-codex-2026-09-05T163615Z-417f07db9a2c-r1 (gpt-5.6-terra) — verdict REJECT, two findings, both substantive. F1 is the sharper: the decision requires every third-party clause to carry a forty-hex pin with a version comment in an exact grammar, makes that an observable acceptance criterion, and names a policy map plus one linter invocation as the complete enforcement. The linter's documented default persona does not report a missing version comment at all; only its pedantic persona does, and even that does not establish the exact comment grammar, its own test data accepting a comment without the required prefix. So the format, the acceptance criterion and the gate disagree, and the decision must either add a check that enforces the grammar or narrow the criterion to what the declared command actually checks. F2 is an evidence-integrity finding: the decision claims the saved script reproduces the whole evidence log, and the auditor reran it and found three addenda materially reduced. The conclusions themselves reproduced independently, and the auditor confirmed the decision's counts against the pinned trees, so the recommendation is not challenged; the preservation claim is simply false as written. Both findings are the programme's recurring defect in different clothes: a claim wider than its evidence, once about what a tool checks and once about what a script reproduces.

CROSS-ITEM CONSISTENCY QUESTION RAISED BY R64, for the controller and not the owner: R64 records that its runner-up wins if the strict reading of fitness gate 2 that R63 applied is also applied to dependencies here. Two items in the same area should not read one gate differently. Both are queued for audit; the auditors are the right place to catch a divergence, and if both pass with different readings the controller must reconcile them before publication rather than let two published decisions disagree about a gate.
PROVISIONAL CONTROLLER RULING on fitness gate 2, raised by R64 and confirmed against R63. The divergence is real and specific. R63 excluded a candidate at gate 2 for "undeclared MSRV" and R64's recommended tokenizer declares no rust-version and is passed with a qualification, so the two items apply different rules to the same gate. Left alone, two published decisions in one area would disagree about a gate that every crate item is measured on.
The gate's text is: "crate and dependency-tree MSRV within `stable minus 2 minor versions, raised only in a minor release, declared as rust-version in Cargo.toml and tested in CI`". Its subject is a value, the MSRV, and "within" compares that value against the window the quoted policy defines. The quoted string is the registry's msrv-policy, described there as the template's own minimum supported Rust version rule, so its trailing clauses about declaring rust-version and testing in CI state what the TEMPLATE must do, not what every dependency must do. Reading them as requirements on candidates converts a window comparison into a documentation requirement the gate does not state.
Ruling, provisional and open to any auditor or the owner overturning it: a candidate with no declared rust-version does not thereby fail gate 2. It fails only if its effective MSRV is shown to exceed the floor. Where the effective MSRV cannot be determined, the candidate is recorded as GATE 2 UNPROVEN with what would determine it, which is a weakness to weigh against it and not an automatic exclusion. This is the treatment the programme already gives every other unproven claim.
Expected consequences, which the audits must test rather than assume. R63's recommendation is unlikely to move, because its recommended crate declares a floor comfortably inside the window and passes the gate on its own evidence; what changes is the framing of one candidate it excluded. R64's recommended tokenizer becomes gate-2 unproven rather than a qualified pass, and its decision must say so; whether that changes its pick against the runner-up, which declares a floor and has a matching CI job, is exactly what R64's audit was asked to weigh. Both audits carry the question; if either auditor argues the strict reading is correct, this ruling is withdrawn and both items are revised the other way.

Then it found two things neither raw had: one crate's dynamic arrangement mode BYPASSES that measurement function entirely and uses the width crate directly, so for that mode the question is unsettled and is carried into Engines as the implementation phase's first check; and that crate's own test for the behaviour never compiles because of a configuration contradiction, which explains a named upstream issue. That is the difference between settling a question and settling the part of it your evidence reaches. It also applied today's reproducibility contract properly: immutable published tarballs asserted by hash, fixed commit identifiers, drifting JSON snapshotted into an evidence subdirectory, then a rerun diffed against the saved log returning empty. No performance figure is asserted anywhere.
FORWARD COUPLING recorded for R66's synthesis: R85 places a requirement on R66 to enable the table crate's ANSI-width feature, because R85's recommendation depends on that feature being on and R66 owns the output surface that selects it. R66's synthesizer must be told, since R66's own reports predate this.
DEFECT IN THE CONTROLLER'S OWN TOOLING, found by checking rather than by waiting: the serial Codex audit queue stalled for roughly twenty-eight minutes with fourteen jobs waiting and NO audit running. Cause: the queue serialises on a lock directory whose holder removes it on completion, and one holder exited without removing it, so every waiter blocked on a lock nobody held. Nothing was lost, because each waiter polls rather than failing, but the whole audit critical path was idle. Found by asking why zero Codex processes were running while fourteen waiters existed; a status line reporting "fourteen queued" would have looked healthy. Fixed by clearing the stale lock, after confirming no audit was running, and by arming a bounded watchdog that clears a lock only when no audit process has existed for two consecutive checks, so it can never interrupt a running audit. The queue resumed immediately.
Related measurement, and the reason the queue stays single-lane: swap is at 21.3 of 22.5 GB used with 39 percent of memory free. Two concurrent Codex audits have already been killed for memory on this machine once, and this is not the moment to try again. The audits stay serial.
Task (R33): revision 2 (claude-opus-5), sha256 1bf96b43e0198e07bccca2ca4ecf0d5a6a2afb939260d896b8eca4814757a068. A new check walks each candidate's required dependencies at the pinned version, resolving each requirement by the package manager's own caret rule, and the result is stronger than the finding: the named substitute fails, and so do BOTH originally recommended members, one on a single dependency and one on five, while the other capability's six all resolve dual so it is not disqualified and is explicitly not cleared either. So under the strict reading the programme has no proven snapshot stack at all, which is a materially bigger consequence than "the named substitute was wrong", and the decision states the four things a resolved-tree audit would have to settle. On the reproducibility finding it rewrote the script with pipefail, per-command status capture, one hundred and eighty-six content and status assertions and a failure counter as the exit status, folded in the three hand-run follow-ups, corrected the wrong path, resolved repository paths from the script's own location and preserved check numbering so every existing citation still lands. The rerun diff is identical apart from run timestamps and one live counter that moved by one in seventy seconds, and the decision states a two-tier comparison rule for exactly that. Its enumeration found thirty-nine locations across the two findings and it reported honestly that two fell outside its first pattern and were caught by a widened one. Protected regions byte-identical by diff against the revision-1 blob.
Task (R65) and Task (R81): both synthesis agents STALLED after writing their decisions, their final replies never arriving. The decisions are complete and correct on disk, which is the whole point of the repository being the state: four H2 sections in order, a re-verify line, four parameter lines with no registry mismatch, and full evidence directories. Both applied today's reproducibility contract without being told twice: R65 snapshotted the three volatile specification pages it cites and saved its generated parameter block and its diff; R81 saved a resolved lockfile and a licences dump for the generator's whole tree, which is precisely the resolved-tree walk R33 could not do for its own candidates. Audits queued from the decisions themselves rather than from any agent summary.
CONTROLLER TOOLING FAILURE, diagnosed properly after the owner asked why work was not finishing. The owner's question was whether Doxa had a problem. It does not, and no research was outstanding: a sweep of every manifest shows exactly one engine operation not in the succeeded state across all thirty-two Wave 0 items, R66's Doxa operation, which has been in the partial state since it failed on 2026-09-09 and is not running. No Doxa process exists on this machine. The real problem was mine. The serial Codex audit queue serialises on a lock directory that each job removes on completion, and jobs were leaving it behind, so waiters blocked on a lock nobody held. Measured cost: audits started at 07:49, 08:09, 08:18, 08:27, 08:32, 09:00, 09:05 and 09:09, then NOTHING until 11:02. One hour and fifty-three minutes idle with fourteen jobs queued. The watchdog armed earlier did clear three stale locks but was not sufficient. Fixed by removing the mechanism rather than patching it: the fourteen waiters and the watchdog are stopped, and one sequential runner process now executes the remaining thirteen audits from a jobs file, in order, one at a time. A single process cannot race itself, so there is no lock and nothing to go stale. The audit already running was left alone and completed normally.
SECOND-ORDER LESSON, and the more useful one: every status line I produced while this was happening was true and useless. "Fourteen audits queued" describes a healthy pipeline and a stalled one identically. The scanner has the same flaw in a different place: it reports R66's failed Doxa operation as "collect in-flight", which reads as work in progress when the operation failed two days ago. A status report must distinguish work that is running from work that is merely not finished.
CORRECTION TO AN EARLIER LEDGER ENTRY AND TO WHAT THE CONTROLLER TOLD THE OWNER (R43): the entry for R43's decision praised it for settling a confidence downgrade by reading the cited issue, reporting that the issue was a feature request its own reporter closed within minutes and never mentioned the mechanism. The empirical audit read that issue and the substantive half of that is FALSE. The procedural facts hold, but the issue body states under a Current Behavior heading that the mechanism also does not display visibly, and identifies a version; a later comment does not withdraw it. So the other engine's narrower claim, that a user report alleges a visibility failure in one past version, is supported by the very issue used to dismiss it. The controller repeated this to the owner as an example of good work. It was an error propagated from the decision without checking, which is exactly the defect the programme keeps catching in others. Revision 2 is dispatched to correct the decision and to reconsider any confidence level resting on the withdrawn argument.

Task (R17): empirical audit r1 — verdict REJECT. The finding is one this programme has not seen before and is worth recording as a class: a recorded provenance assertion does not rerun, because the verification command exits 1 when no valid signing verifier can be initialised in this environment. So the decision recorded a check that cannot pass here as though it had passed. That is not a claim wider than its evidence; it is a check whose failure mode is environmental, and the honest repair is either to record the assertion as unproven with the environment it needs, or to verify provenance by a means that works here.

Task (R19): empirical audit r1 — verdict REJECT, and the first finding is a REVERSAL rather than a qualification, which makes it the most valuable audit result of the session so far. The decision rejects the TypeScript repository's update-type filter on the ground that the update tool uses plain semantic versioning, so a group admitting minor updates would admit a pre-1.0 bump the package manager treats as incompatible. The auditor read the pinned source and found the opposite: the tool routes pre-1.0 updates to a Cargo-specific classifier that returns the MAJOR category when the 0.y component changes, the pinned test asserts exactly that for 0.1.0 to 0.2.0, and the group-update path admits an update only when the group's declared types contain the computed category. So a Cargo group declaring minor and patch would NOT admit that bump, and one of the recommendation's two pillars rests on a mechanism that does not exist. The auditor confirms the other pillar holds, the unconditional production-dependency predicate being real, so the rejection of the role taxonomy stands on its own evidence. Revision 2 is briefed that a recommendation whose stated reason was false must be re-argued or changed, not quietly repaired, and that it must decide the item again on the evidence that survives. Second finding: the saved check does not replay the decision's named source pins.
NOTE ON WHY THIS ONE WAS CAUGHT: the audit brief for R19 named that exact mechanism claim and told the auditor to verify it in the tool's own source at the named pin, because the decision rested on it. Three of today's most valuable audit findings came from briefs that named the load-bearing claim and told the auditor where to look. A brief that says only "audit this decision" gets a decision-shaped review; a brief that names the pillar gets the pillar tested.

Task (R17): revision 2 (claude-opus-5), sha256 e2c3d588eaf1a098027b4c9ced4fa01ef1341fd27ef4cfdfa012bfc44a8321ec, and it is the best diagnosis the programme has produced. The controller's brief said the provenance check fails in "this environment". The revision refused that framing and found the actual mechanism: the verification command builds its verifier from trusted-root metadata it refreshes into a cache directory, so ANY process whose cache path is unwritable exits 1 with the observed message, before it ever touches the artifact. The tool was present, the trust root reachable and the credentials valid. It then PROVED the diagnosis by reproducing the auditor's exact result, running the unmodified saved script with the cache directory made unwritable and getting twenty-two passes and the single failure the auditor saw, and it identified why revision 1 passed: the auditing sandbox is such a process and the synthesizing shell was not. That also answers the reach question with evidence rather than inspection, because the same run proves no other assertion shares the dependency.
It took the harder repair, making the check work in the failing environment by redirecting the trusted-root cache into its own working directory, and added two negative controls that execute both prerequisites, one proving the unwritable cache causes the failure and one proving an unauthenticated run fails differently, which turns into a named requirement that the implementation phase supply a token. It rejected the easier repair with a reason worth keeping: the command genuinely verifies once the cache is writable, so recording the assertion as unproven would understate the evidence as badly as revision 1 overstated it. And it narrowed a claim nobody had questioned, establishing that the repository flag enforces repository identity only while two further properties are read from the certificate rather than enforced, naming the flag the implementation phase should pass to enforce them. Ten provenance-bearing locations found, eight edited, two deliberately left with stated reasons. Protected regions verbatim unchanged with zero differing lines, and the evidence log matches none of ten credential patterns.
CONTROLLER DISCIPLINE, fourth occurrence and the last: commit c239b3a swept a mid-edit snapshot of R17's decision into HEAD. I recorded the explicit-paths rule one commit earlier and then did not follow it. The rule is not the problem; following it is. Every commit from here lists paths, and no commit uses -A while any agent is running.

Task (R26): empirical audit r1 — verdict REJECT on one finding, with the two load-bearing claims CONFIRMED. The auditor verified the central claim at the installed binary's own pin, reproduced the healthy guard and both injected faults, confirmed the decision correctly rejected a raw report's obsolete citation by showing the old path returns 404 at the pin while the new one returns 200, and confirmed the parameter lines and the honest platform scoping, including that the decision records macOS-only execution and states the Linux half as a future acceptance criterion rather than a proved claim. The finding is that three cited phases are not in the saved script: it ends at phase 7, while the decision relies on phases 8, 9 and 10 for stream separation, the packaged-manifest projection and the stale-archive hazard, and the cited log preserves their outputs rather than the commands. So those three claims are recorded observations without a reproducible check. This is the fifth reproducibility rejection today and the pattern is now unmistakable: synthesizers run follow-up checks by hand and cite them without folding them into the archived script. The contract already requires folding them in; this one predates that clause.
Task (R26): revision 2 (claude-opus-5), sha256 f9f85f7b31694ed30bb44232eed6207f217d2e92716f4d59266687c6bab12496. It integrated the three missing phases as CODE in the archived script rather than as an addendum, with pipefail, an assert helper that compares expected CONTENT rather than exit codes, a failure counter, and the counter as the script's exit status. Twenty-two passes, no failures, exit 0, log regenerated, and an immediate second run diffed line for line against the cited log with the only differences being header paths and the toolchain's own compressed byte counts, stated as the comparison rule.
AND IT VINDICATED THE CONTRACT CLAUSE IN THE ACT OF OBEYING IT. The clause added today says a check that cannot fail is not evidence, and requires assertions on content rather than on exit codes. The first run of the newly assertive script FAILED one assertion, and the failure uncovered a fact nobody had: the toolchain lists packaged paths in byte order rather than in locale collation. That is a new result, now recorded and carried into the implementation-phase constraints. A check written to pass would have hidden it; a check written to be capable of failing found it on its first run. This is the strongest evidence so far that the three contract fixes made today are earning their cost rather than merely adding ceremony.
Enumeration found eleven locations and edited ten, leaving one that was re-read and remains correct. Confirmed regions verified byte-identical by diff against the revision-1 blob, and the earlier phases keep their log line numbers so every existing citation into them carries forward verified, which is a detail that would have broken silently had the phases been renumbered.
RULING WITHDRAWN: fitness gate 2 is read strictly. I ruled provisionally that an undeclared rust-version makes a candidate UNPROVEN at gate 2 rather than failed, weighed against it but not excluding, on the ground that the gate compares a version against a window and the declaration and continuous-integration clauses belong to the template's own obligations. I put that ruling to both affected items' auditors and said that if either argued for the strict reading it would be withdrawn. BOTH argued for it, independently, and their argument is better than mine. The prompt's gate 2 quotes the policy in full, declaration and CI testing included, and the instruction that follows says every candidate is gate-checked before popularity is weighed and that a failed gate excludes the candidate. So an unknown effective version is not evidence of incompatibility, as I said, but it is insufficient evidence to PASS a gate the prompt says must be passed. That distinction is the part I got wrong: I treated the gate as a test the candidate could fail, when the prompt writes it as a bar the candidate must clear.
Consequences, both now briefed. R63's exclusions stand exactly as recorded and its decision needs no change on this point. R64 must change its selection to the runner-up it already recorded, which declares a version and has a matching continuous-integration job, because its current pick declares none. The two items will now read the gate the same way, which was the point of raising it. The owner briefing is corrected to record the withdrawal rather than the ruling.
WHAT THIS SAYS ABOUT THE PROCESS, and it is the reason the ruling was made provisionally rather than silently: a controller decision that changes two recommendations should not rest on the controller's own reading alone. Putting it in both audit briefs cost nothing, produced two independent readings of the prompt text, and reversed me within one audit round. Had I ruled silently, two published decisions would have applied a gate in a way the programme's own prompts do not support.
AUDIT BATCH RESULT, 2026-09-11. Six empirical approvals landed together: R25 revision 7, R17 revision 2, R21 revision 2, R33 revision 2, R43 revision 2 and R45 revision 2, each with no unresolved findings. Every one of those six had been rejected at least once, and three of them twice, so the revision discipline is converging rather than circling. Two further rejections: R19 revision 2 on a rerun-and-diff record that contradicts its own cited log, and R26 revision 2 on a cross-run comparison rule that excludes observed non-header differences. Both are the same family as the five reproducibility rejections before them, and both are now second-order: the decisions did rerun and diff as the contract requires, and stated the rule imprecisely. That is progress, from not checking at all to checking and describing the check loosely, but it is still a claim wider than its evidence.
STATE AT THIS POINT: sixteen of thirty-two Wave 0 items published. Seven items are one judgment audit from publication. Eight need a revision. One, R66, is blocked on an owner spend decision. Four owner decisions are outstanding and recorded in docs/planning/p02/owner-decisions.md.

Task (R34): judgment audit r1 by audit-fable-2026-09-05T165440Z-d2e35904dc88-r1 (claude-opus-5, family anthropic) — verdict APPROVE, unresolved-findings none. It confirmed the override is stated where an implementer will meet it, in the recommendation table cell and not only in the analysis, and judged the self-narrowing right rather than over-cautious, checking all three limits and finding the hazard intact: nine packages locked with one held back, invisible in the job result though visible in the log, and a small class that is nonetheless where the fastest-moving crates sit.
THE ONE NOTE P03 MUST CARRY (R34, non-blocking but material): the decision's central chain was executed on a SINGLE-PACKAGE manifest. The toolchain's own reference states that the edition has no effect on the resolver used in a workspace and that the resolver must be set explicitly in a virtual workspace. So the edition-to-resolver premise does NOT carry automatically to the workspace shape the implementation phase will actually build. The auditor approved because the override stays required-or-harmless either way, which is correct, but a decision whose headline finding rests on a fixture shape the template will not have is exactly the kind of thing that looks settled and is not. Recorded here and in the decision's own unproven list rather than left in an audit nobody rereads.
Two further non-blocking notes: the bolded headline sentence says the lane re-tests the same graph where the evidence is that eight of nine packages moved, corrected two lines later but not in the line most likely to be quoted alone; and one baseline-review line is keyed to two ledger rows where the runbook form takes one.

Task (R21): PUBLISHED, acceptance sha256 82d673c3838681901cfe8458b9409895efab3ec296de7bd1c7517ff35206349b. Its judgment audit approved and did something none of the others did: it EXERCISED the decision's own comparison rule. Task (R25): PUBLISHED after SEVEN revisions, acceptance sha256 7605e8efdcbe58611b91ebb60666d31cb21c84b31b0b65c893451c99d11c53b9, twenty-two staged changes.
