Task (R33): PUBLISHED, acceptance sha256 ba9b17c23454c8ba6930e3cb1571b8f70c1c265128dc1161d85cf60ab583e0fe.
CORRECTION TO THE OWNER BRIEFING, and it was my error: I told the owner three items depend on the licence ruling and that the strict branch leaves no proven snapshot stack. R33's judgment auditor established that R33 does NOT depend on the ruling. Its crates carry Apache-2.0, which is one arm of the fixed license value, so they pass on the registry's own wording without the contested never-linked argument at all. The no-stack outcome belongs to a THIRD, stricter reading that nobody has adopted, under which every dependency at every depth must carry the dual expression. The ruling's real reach is R42's install backend, which carries a copyleft licence and genuinely is excluded under the plain reading, plus any future item recommending a copyleft tool. I widened the question to the owner without first checking the candidates' licence values against the registry's own wording. The briefing is corrected.
DOXA DIAGNOSTIC, 2026-09-11, at the owner's request, written to docs/planning/p02/doxa-diagnostic.md and reconstructed from disk and the providers' own APIs rather than from any session's memory. Findings in brief. Nine Doxa operations were ever submitted, all on 2026-09-08 and 09; eight succeeded and every one of the eight reports is on disk, integrated and cited; the ninth is R66. No paid research is uncollected and none was lost. Resumption demonstrably worked: R49's operation completed around 08:1x UTC on 09-09 and was collected at 00:54 UTC on 09-10 by a process that had not submitted it. Two orphaned provider jobs existed and both are now closed out: a 2026-09-05 attempt at R38 left an OpenAI job that FAILED on credit_balance_exhausted with zero tokens, costing nothing and producing nothing, and R66's OpenAI job completed at 175,816 tokens and was recovered at the time. Recorded spend USD 36.5 to 61.5 of the 300 ceiling, single attempt per operation, no duplicate submissions.
THE OWNER'S PREMISE, CORRECTED IN BOTH DIRECTIONS. Nothing was forgotten across a disconnection and no batch was left running unattended; there was never a large batch to leave. But the expectation of broad parallelism was right and unmet, for a reason that is an owner decision rather than a fault: twenty-six items require Doxa, only nine have ever been ADMITTED to the programme, and all nine of those had Doxa submitted. The seventeen that look missing are Wave 1 and later, held by the Wave 0 review gate. Within Wave 0 the parallelism was complete.
THE REAL FAILURE IS MINE AND IT IS REPORTING. R66's operation failed on 2026-09-09 and my scanner described it as "collect in-flight" for two days, which reads as work in progress; a dead operation and a live one were indistinguishable in every status report the owner received. Fixed now: the scanner separates live operations from stopped ones and prints "STOPPED, needs a decision" for partial, failed or unknown states. Two further reporting gaps recorded: there was no Doxa inventory at all until this question forced one, and I never said plainly that Doxa's checkpoint store is useless as an inventory, holding 1,084 files of which 838 are a sibling repository's test noise and exactly nine are ours, and being machine-local besides.
OWNER DECISION RECORDED, 2026-09-11 (Q1, answered B). The owner settled the SHIPPING half of R18 and added requirements the research had only hoped for. The two workflow files ship with the template. An unconfigured clone MUST produce a skipped job and MUST NOT produce a red check; that was R18's unproven item U2 and it is now an acceptance criterion the implementation phase must prove before the files ship. The enablement step must be documented. And on the owner's explicit choice of option B, the template adds a small always-run preflight job that prints the reason for skipping and gates the real job behind it, rather than relying on a silent grey skipped job; the owner accepted its cost, one runner-minute per triggering event on every clone. This supersedes nothing in R18's recommendation, which already recommended shipping both files inert; it strengthens it by converting a design intention into a testable requirement and by choosing visible skipping over silent skipping.
Clarification the owner needed and the controller failed to give: the brief said "Claude GitHub App installation token" without explaining it, which reasonably read as asking the owner to install a GitHub App. No App installation is asked of anyone. The credential path is workload identity federation: an adopter sets three federation identifiers as repository VARIABLES, there is no API key and no secret, and the action itself exchanges an OIDC token for the App installation token at runtime. The controller also owed the owner the constraint that makes their requested design possible at all: a job-level condition cannot read secrets, verified both in the platform's context table and by actionlint rejecting it, so a credential-presence gate is expressible ONLY because the federation identifiers are variables.
OWNER AMENDMENT, 2026-09-12 (supersedes the open question Q2 and amends published R18). The owner gave direction rather than answering the residual question as posed, and the direction resolves it: the two Claude workflows SHIP BY DEFAULT and run whenever the credential they need is present, with no enable variable at all. If the credential is absent they do not run, do not error, and print that the key needs setting. A user who does not want them deletes the files. Both lanes must reject bots. The author-trust test stays the ASSOCIATION check R18 already specifies; the owner considered a genuine write-permission check and chose association as the more accurate test, so that requirement is withdrawn before any work was done on it. The owner also wants a Codex equivalent of both workflows to exist in the template.
This amends published R18 in three ways and the item must be reopened for a revision carrying a `## Supersedes` section, which the approved spec permits for exactly this. First, the gate changes from `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_REVIEW` and `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT` to credential presence. Second, the assistant lane gains bot exclusion, which it does not have today: the review-lane fixture contains two bot checks and the assistant fixture contains none, and R18's own research found the action's internal write check returns true unconditionally for any actor whose login ends in `[bot]`, so the owner's instruction closes a real gap rather than restating one. Third, the enablement question R18 made conditional is answered by the design rather than by an acceptance: there is no flag for the owner to set, so there is no separate act of enabling to accept.
THE MECHANISM, and the owner's earlier preflight choice is what makes it possible. A job-level condition cannot read secrets; that is verified twice, against the platform's context table and by actionlint rejecting it. So credential-presence gating is NOT expressible as a one-line job condition when the credential is a secret. Secrets ARE available in a job's env block, so the always-run preflight job the owner chose on 2026-09-11 reads the credential into an environment value, prints the skip reason, and publishes an output both real jobs gate on through `needs`. The owner chose that preflight for visibility a day before it turned out to be the only way to satisfy this instruction.
STILL OUTSTANDING AND ASKED TWICE: whether the owner has admin access to an Anthropic organisation. It is a fact, not a decision, and it determines which credential the preflight detects. Federation uses three repository VARIABLES, readable directly in a job condition, which would make the preflight trivial and would let the gate be a one-liner after all. An API key or OAuth token is a SECRET, which is the case that requires the preflight job. The owner's instruction reads as the secret path. The revision cannot be written correctly without this answer, because it changes both the detection code and the setup documentation every adopter follows.
NEW SCOPE: a Codex equivalent of both workflows. R18's binding prompt is scoped to the Claude action, so this cannot be folded into its revision without breaking the prompt contract. It needs its own item with its own prompt: different vendor, different credential, different trigger vocabulary, different permission model, and its own version of the trusted-author residual.
NEW ITEM REGISTERED, 2026-09-12: R86 codex-assisted-review-workflows, bundle, Deep tier with engines codex, opus and doxa, matching R18's tier because it is the same class of decision. Created on the owner's direction that a Codex equivalent of the two Claude workflows exist in the template. R18's binding prompt is scoped to the Claude action, so this could not be folded into its revision without breaking the prompt contract. The prompt carries the eight required H2 sections in order, names R18, R20 and R45 as couplings with what each owns so this item does not decide them, and states the owner's standing requirements as constraints: ship by default with no enable variable, run when the credential is present, do not error when it is absent and print that the key needs setting, a user who does not want them deletes the files, both lanes reject bots, and the author-trust test is an association check and not a write-permission check. Origin is recorded as `none` and the verdict as RUST-ONLY because no Codex workflow exists in either source repository. Two ledger rows, F361 and F362, were added to docs/port/COMMONALITY.md under the ci-workflows area, because the tree checker fails any non-dropped index item with no ledger row. scripts/check-research-tree.sh prints OK.
NEW OWNER ITEM, and the authoring agent was right to refuse it: scripts/check-research-tree.sh --require-owner-review now fails with "owner review: R86 has 0 rows, expected exactly 1". docs/port/OWNER-REVIEW.md records an eighty-four-item codesign collected 2026-09-04, and a disposition for an item created eight days later can only come from the owner. The agent declined to invent one, which is correct: fabricating an owner review row would forge the one record in the programme that exists to prove the owner saw an item. The ordinary structural check passes; only the stricter owner-review variant fails, so nothing is blocked today, but R86 cannot publish without that row.
Task (R18): revision 6 (claude-opus-5), sha256 cd3c7add1eba27bae8d1bfe5070d9ebd82761e12dbd876ca6bed99b52577cf99, implementing the owner's 2026-09-12 direction and carrying a `## Supersedes` section, which the approved spec permits for a reversal. It replaced the enable variables with a credential-presence preflight, using the mechanism the owner had chosen a day earlier: a job-level condition cannot read secrets but a job's env block can, so the preflight reads both credential shapes into env, prints the missing keys as a notice and to the step summary, exits 0, and publishes a literal that the real jobs gate on through `needs`.
THE BEST THING IN IT IS A BUG IT FOUND IN ITSELF. The gate was first written as `!= 'none'` and corrected to a positive predicate, because a SKIPPED preflight publishes an empty output and the empty string is not equal to 'none', so the negative form would have run the real job in exactly the case the gate exists to prevent. It also proved the relay is type-checked by mutating an output name and watching actionlint reject it. Both new fixtures lint clean, the negative control still exits 1 on the secrets diagnostic.
It corrected the controller twice. Federation is detected on TWO identifiers, not three: isWorkloadIdentityConfigured() requires the rule and organisation identifiers and action.yml marks the service-account identifier optional. The controller had told the owner three, repeatedly. And three revision-number attributions in the decision were wrong against git: the enable flag existed at revisions 1 through 5, not 3 through 5. The assistant lane gained the bot exclusion it never had, five checks against zero in every earlier assistant fixture. Enumeration found fifty-five locations across four searches, the fourth of which caught one the three mandated searches missed, and edited thirty-eight.
CONTROLLER FINDING ON REVISION 6, to be fixed before audit: the `## Supersedes` section embeds revision 5 VERBATIM, which reproduces its four H2 headings at document level and takes the file from about 180 KB to 360402 bytes. The packager's parser tolerates it, the four parameter lines still resolve correctly and the structure check passes, but the approved spec says a decision has exactly its four H2 sections plus Supersedes, and a document with nine H2 headings is not that. Revision 5 is already preserved in git history and in the published copy under research/topics/, so embedding it buys nothing and costs every future reader and auditor. Being sent back for a Supersedes section that states what it supersedes and why, and cites the published copy by path and hash, rather than reproducing it.
CONTROLLER FINDING WITHDRAWN, and it was wrong on the merits. I sent revision 6 back because its `## Supersedes` section embedded revision 5 verbatim, doubling the file to 360402 bytes and putting nine H2 headings in a document the spec describes as having five. My reasoning was that revision 5 is preserved in git history and in the published copy, so embedding it bought nothing. That reasoning is wrong, and the synthesizer established it by RUNNING THE PUBLISHER rather than arguing with me: scripts/research_runner.py check_history requires the published file's current bytes to appear verbatim after the `## Supersedes` marker and raises "decision revision must preserve prior DECISION.md bytes verbatim under ## Supersedes" otherwise, and it is called on the publication target twice. docs/planning/p02/runner-guide.md states the same requirement in prose. I verified both in source myself before accepting the correction. So the embed is MANDATORY, not redundant, and the guard exists for a reason I had not considered: it puts the superseded text physically inside the published artifact, so a reader of a published decision holds the prior text without needing git.
The three options the synthesizer offered were to change check_history and the runner guide to accept a hash citation, to keep the embed for publication and the prose form in the run copy only, or to publish through recovery with the gate waived. All three are wrong for the same reason: they change or evade a guard to suit a preference of mine about file size. Restored revision 6 to its original bytes from commit c9bf1c4, sha256 cd3c7add1eba27bae8d1bfe5070d9ebd82761e12dbd876ca6bed99b52577cf99, and verified check_history now passes against the live published copy.
The synthesizer's second point resolves itself with the restore, and it was right to raise it rather than quietly fix it. Removing the embed had falsified three sentences inside the four approved sections and one block of a captured evidence log, all of which describe whole-file counts that are true only with the embed present; the assumes-line count is 8 again, which is what those sentences say. Had I insisted on the prose form, an auditor would have found three false statements and a stale capture, and the synthesizer told me so in advance instead of letting it happen.
LESSON, and it is about me rather than the item: I overrode a structural choice on an aesthetic judgement without checking whether the programme's own publisher required it, having just spent two days telling synthesizers not to assert what they had not executed. The synthesizer executed the check I should have run before sending the work back.
Task (R17): judgment audit r2 — verdict REJECT, one finding, and it is the most consequential audit finding of the programme so far because it would have shipped a broken security gate. The decision states its gate rule five times and never says what to do with an exit code outside the three the scanner documents. Since the default shell for a workflow step exits on error, an implementer handles exit codes by hand, and the rule as written, gate on the specific success code and never on a non-zero exit, produces a job that PASSES when the scanner fails to run. That is fail-open on the one rule the decision itself calls load-bearing, and no acceptance check covered it. Revision 3 must state that exits outside the documented set are tool errors and must fail the job, and add an implementation-phase check for exactly that case.
THIRD SCANNER BLIND SPOT, same family as the first two, and the owner's repeated questions are what kept surfacing them. An item reopened for a revision keeps its resolved index row until the revision republishes, so scripts/research_scan.py reported R18 as "done" for an entire session while an unpublished revision 6 sat in its run directory. The first blind spot reported a failed provider operation as in flight; the second named the in-flight engine and hid the ones that never started; this one hides an entire reopened item. All three were status lines that were true and useless. Fixed: the scanner now compares a resolved item's run decision against its published copy and prints "REOPENED: run is at revision N, published copy differs". A genuinely finished item still reads done, verified against R85.
Task (R49): revision 2 (claude-opus-5), sha256 1b5a6b99ecedb88f7358d16a4567aa9b56838a2102e9e4feda99d00bc787513f, self-committed at 2ac02e1. The audit said the owned parameter overstated lockfile inclusion as unconditional. The revision did not soften the claim; it established the actual condition at the toolchain tag matching the installed version and wrote it into the owned value: the lockfile IS included regardless of what include or exclude says, and so are the manifest and any licence file, but NOT unconditionally, because `cargo package --exclude-lockfile` omits it and `cargo publish` has no such flag, so this template never passes it. That is a materially more useful statement than either the original overstatement or a hedge, and it is the kind of correction only a source read produces. Established from the manifest reference, the package command documentation, both installed help outputs and five source locations, plus a whole-tarball enumeration proving exactly three files mention the internal flag and exactly two assign it. Thirteen assertions exit 0, the rerun differs only on a retrieval timestamp, and three negative controls each fail as intended. Enumeration found five locations carrying the claim and edited all five, plus a narrowed preamble sentence that had said no package command was run when two checks print its help. Confirmed regions proven unchanged by comparing line-set hashes rather than by eye.
Task (R17): revision 3 (claude-opus-5), sha256 7e4e27e6d26577a6c1180cff7efea7bf9d755cdb247023aafd7a4b1f622eab19, self-committed. It answered the fail-open finding by EXECUTION rather than by assertion: it verified the scanner's documented exit codes itself at a named tag, then EXECUTED two codes outside that set, one from a flag the top-level command rejects and one from a scan whose target is missing, proving the gap was reachable rather than theoretical, and it proved GitHub's default step shell is bash with the error flag, which is why the step must capture the code at all. The rule is now an allow-list stated once in full and referenced from six other locations, with a new acceptance check requiring that a scanner replaced by a false command, or a rejected flag, must fail the job. Its enumeration found seven locations where the auditor named five; the two extra were an acceptance criterion and a per-principle paragraph.
It answered the "does this gap exist elsewhere" question both ways, which is the honest shape: YES once, because the ranked runner-up was gated only by its own single finding code, the identical shape, so the allow-list is now tool-owned and must be re-derived at the runner-up's own pin since its codes were never executed here; and NO once, because the provenance check asserts exactly one code, an allow-list of size one, and is therefore fail-closed.
LATENT DEFECT IT FOUND THAT BOTH AUDITS PASSED OVER, and it is the most interesting failure in the programme's own review machinery. Revision 2's check script captured the scanner's exit-code table with a range expression that stops at the first blank line after the heading, so the cited evidence log NEVER CONTAINED the table the 0/200/205 figure came from. The empirical audit approved that revision and the judgment audit rejected it on a different ground; neither noticed that the load-bearing figure's evidence was absent from the log it cited. Two independent audits can both miss a missing capture, because each was checking whether claims matched the log rather than whether the log contained what the claim cited. The section now prints the whole table and the file's digest. Also fixed: two checks first read a moving branch and are now pinned.
Task (R19): revision 3 (claude-opus-5), sha256 ec37d000455e786370406944a42db27b7bd4b3e62ad73c970a93e88058e30ad9. The finding was that the decision's rerun-and-diff record contradicted its cited log, and the truth is worse than a contradiction: THE DESCRIBED RUN DOES NOT EXIST ON DISK. Revision 2 claimed a rerun at one timestamp with 919 lines diffing to ten lines with one content difference; the cited log records a different timestamp, 935 lines, and carries a TLS handshake timeout in two PINNED sections. The root cause is the defect the contract added a clause for: one section routes a failed read to a note and the other has no assertion at all, so a network failure blanked both without failing the run. The revision reran the unmodified script twice, with the script body byte-identical across all three logs, got twenty-nine passes and no failures with both reads succeeding, and published a four-part comparison: script identity, immutable sections identical at 285 lines each side, exactly two hunks both being the transport reads, and nine timestamp lines differing between two consecutive runs.
It also caught TWO overclaims of its own before reporting them, which is the behaviour the programme wants and rarely gets: its count of unasserted pinned reads was wrong, an enumeration finding five unasserted and one note-only rather than two; and its description of why six survey rows vanished was wrong, the cause being that the surveyed repository opened a hundred pull requests in four days so its six fell off the first page, with both cited group branches still present and re-confirmed by a second-page query.
CONTROLLER DESIGN ERROR, reported by the agent that hit it: nine revision agents were dispatched into ONE worktree and several of them self-commit, so concurrent writers share a branch. One agent's `git commit --amend` landed on another agent's commit and rewrote its message; it detected this, restored the original bit-identically with a soft reset, and re-committed its own delta separately. Verified after the fact: R17's commit 1c90fd45 is reachable with its original subject, both decisions match the hashes their agents reported, and the committed blobs equal the working tree for both. The dangling commits git fsck reports are the pre-amend versions and hold nothing absent from history. Nothing was lost, and that is luck plus one agent's care rather than design. Standing rule from here: revision agents do not amend, and preferably do not commit at all - the controller commits, which is what the role contract already implies by telling agents to write only their own files.
Task (R26): revision 3 (claude-opus-5), sha256 013fe4c3fc992d7e3755822cd08fe134cafebf83ecff509000b3a98780e8e7f3. This is the strongest reproducibility work the programme has produced, and the reason is that it diagnosed its own previous failure instead of patching the symptom. The audit said revision 2's cross-run comparison rule excluded differences the auditor had observed. Revision 3 established WHY: revision 2 verified its rule in an environment where the other two difference classes CANNOT APPEAR. It then reproduced the auditor's environment deliberately, repointing the scratch directory to produce a packaging-path class and forcing a collation locale to produce a listing-order class, and with those two changes the auditor's own archived 377-line rerun log classifies with zero leftovers.
The rule it wrote is a precondition plus four named classes with their line numbers, and it distinguishes changed PRECONDITIONS from run variance, which is the distinction revision 2 lacked. It then built a CLASSIFIER as an archived script and ran eight comparisons, including against both audit rerun logs and against revision 1's log recovered from a committed git blob, every one reporting zero unclassified lines and zero violations; and it added two negative controls that make the classifier report an unclassified line and a violation, proving the classifier can fail. The original check script and its log are byte-unchanged, so every existing line citation still lands.
Its preservation proof is the most precise yet: a difference engine confirms only seven lines changed, the one hundred and twenty-six unchanged lines are byte-identical with a stated hash, and per-region comparisons are byte-identical for the pruning claim at the installed toolchain's pin, the forty-character pin token, the healthy guard and both injected faults, the obsolete-citation correction, the platform scoping, and the four parameter lines, which also still byte-match the registry. It deliberately left one line alone, having judged it design variance rather than log variance, and said so.
Task (R65): revision 2 (claude-opus-5), sha256 4d428b87131988a74f8d1d4216ff2ac264d38f46733f3bad6c29031f027e150d. IT CORRECTED THE CONTROLLER'S BRIEF: I passed on three findings taken from the audit's truncated headline, and the audit lists FIVE. The agent read the full audit rather than trusting my summary and fixed all five. That is the second time this session a brief of mine has under-reported an audit because I read a headline instead of the document, and the lesson is the same as the one I keep writing into contracts for others.
The first finding is the sharpest and it is a new defect class: the check script had SILENTLY GROWN sixteen assertions since the log was captured, so the decision's stated counts, a number of assertions and a number of sections, were both wrong against the script as it now stood. Neither figure was a lie when written; the script moved underneath them. The script is completed and rerun, and the decision now states the true counts with the citations spread across two pins and nine tags. The second finding defeated the whole point of snapshotting: three unversioned specification pages were asserted against LIVE fetches, so the committed snapshots were outputs of a re-read rather than evidence. The section now reads the committed snapshots, performs no fetch, prints each hash, names path and hash and retrieval time, and marks every quotation as coming from a snapshot; a live refresh is opt-in, writes to separate files and adds no assertion. The third established the resolver's real signature at a tag: three policy inputs, with the stream handle entering later and only choosing how colour is written rather than whether. The remaining two were attribution overreach, narrowed: one crate is now described as a library API model rather than a surveyed command-line tool, and a maintainer attribution now rests on registry ownership of the crate in question, with the endpoint asserted NOT to support the wider claim that was dropped.
Two consecutive runs diff empty at one hundred and seventy-seven assertions and no failures, the saved log being the second run's output and identical to the first. Provenance is stated as the contract asks: no crate source read from a default branch, thirteen tags each asserted to exist, the earlier no-tag disclosure refuted by reading that crate at a real tag, and one remaining version gap named with its cost.
Task (R63): revision 2 (claude-opus-5), sha256 3ff1eef29650b765411f10d8d60a7b5a832f1d5172284c2fe3bce9001aa91615. The omitted sections are now inline code in the one script under the cited log's own titles, and it found that revision 1's helper had COLLAPSED three of them and dropped their sub-labels, which is why the counts never lined up. Pipefail throughout, fetch-to-file everywhere, and one hundred and sixty-eight content assertions replacing exit-code evidence, with absences asserted as zero match counts and the raw exits kept beside them. It PROVED FALSIFIABILITY TWICE, which is what the contract clause asks for and what most items only claim: two of its own draft expectations genuinely failed during authoring, and it archived a deliberate negative control that exits 1 with thirteen failures. The gate-deciding registry facts are asserted, so a registry move now fails the run rather than passing quietly.
Two things it did that I want to keep as practice. First, it DECLINED to edit the ledger, correctly identifying it as append-only and controller-owned with six other items concurrently in flight, and left its entry for me to append rather than racing. That is the right answer to the concurrency hazard another agent hit an hour earlier. Second, it disclosed the one confirmed region it could not leave byte-identical: a count of presence-checked variables moves from thirty-three to thirty-five, a figure the regenerated log contradicts and which the audit itself had declined to state, and it recorded the change in both the preamble and the engines section rather than letting an auditor find it.
Rerun and diff: the log regenerated, then rerun twice at plus thirty-one seconds and plus seven minutes, the raw diff being one line each time, the volatile date stamp, and identical under the stated rule. One hundred and one of one thousand and forty-five lines carry a volatility prefix.
Task (R60): revision 2 (claude-opus-5), sha256 18769c3a522c94ee59c5635a338828781efa9c6bc8aa4e13ac9443a5773c5c2c. It satisfied the standard's requirement by making the canonical documented noun singular and permitting the plural only as a HIDDEN alias, and it verified in the parser's own source that the hidden form exists and the visible form does not, and that the derive layer emits the hidden call verbatim with no magic attribute. Two new acceptance criteria assert all three parts of the rule and the hidden-alias case, the absent criterion being the auditor's precise finding. It pinned the standard with four identifiers, a version, a commit, a blob and a content hash, plus a resolution recipe that works from a fresh clone, and declared every line citation to be a line number in that blob.
FINDING ABOUT A PUBLISHED ITEM: R67 did NOT pin the standard. It cites the document only by absolute path, line and retrieval date, with no version, commit or hash, and never names the version at all; the version figure appears only in one of R67's own audit logs, which is an auditor's note and not a pin. So there was no existing form for R60 to match, and R60 kept R67's citation style while adding the four identifiers R67 omits. The consequence is that a PUBLISHED decision whose entire error and exit-code contract rests on that document cannot be re-verified against the exact bytes it read. That is a real gap in a resolved item and it belongs on the controller's docket, alongside the owner question of whether the standard binds at all.
IT ALSO FIXED A DEFECT THE AUDITOR NEVER RAISED, and it is the kind that propagates: revision 1 cited the standard's org-locked decision log at one line for three separate rows, and that line is a fourth row entirely, R67's exit-code row, copied across. The correct lines are three different ones. A citation copied from a sibling item and never re-read is exactly the defect this programme keeps finding in others' work, and here it was in our own.
OV-01 remains the only override and is explicitly recorded as REQUESTED, NOT GRANTED as of 2026-09-12, naming the conforming alternatives. Conformance to the singular-noun rule is deliberately NOT filed as a second override, on the ground that a non-waivable requirement is a constraint rather than a choice. Both check scripts carry negative controls proving the harness can fail. Two plural occurrences are deliberately kept as quotations, the standard's own counter-example and a source line, and annotated as such.

CONTROLLER PROCESS DEFECT, third occurrence today: my brief said three findings and the audit had four, exactly as my R65 brief said three where the audit had five. A PRECONDITION THE NEXT AUDITOR NEEDS: the revision's new preservation check reads the revision-1 blob from git, so a rerun from a copy without a repository cannot run it, which is how revision 1's auditor worked. Task (R18): judgment audit r6 by audit-fable-2026-09-06T043454Z-6f31d86e5b7d-r6 (claude-opus-5, family anthropic) — verdict REJECT, one finding, plus the direct answer the controller asked for and a security-architecture note for P03.
THE ANSWER TO THE QUESTION THE OWNER MOST NEEDED: revision 6 exposes MORE than revision 5, downstream only. Activation now needs ONE condition, a credential, where revision 5 needed TWO, a credential and a dedicated enable flag. On the origin repository the two are identical, because the flag was true by construction there. The added cost is one always-run preflight job per guard-passing event in a repository that configured nothing, which the decision does state. That is the honest comparison and it was worth commissioning from someone other than the agent that implemented the instruction.
THE RESIDUAL SURVIVED AT FULL STRENGTH, verified independently rather than accepted: the three residual spans are byte-identical to the preserved revision-5 bytes, the sentence calling this an owner risk judgement that is not eliminated is intact and re-asserted, the supersedes block hashes to the published revision exactly, and the bounding list HONESTLY DROPS the enable flag rather than keeping a bound that no longer exists. That last detail is the difference between a revision that removed a control and one that pretended it still had it.
FINDING JA-06, AND THE OWNER NEEDS TO KNOW IT, because it is a case their instruction did not anticipate rather than a fault in it. The decision claims that installing a credential is itself the act of acceptance and that it says so where the installer will see it. Both are FALSE for an adopter whose repository ALREADY CARRIES an Anthropic API key or OAuth token for an unrelated purpose: no act of acceptance is taken, both lanes go live on the next qualifying event, and the preflight prints NOTHING, because its notice fires only when no credential shape is found. The fix is small and does not change the owner's mechanism: one paragraph on what installing a credential costs, one line in each fixture's adopter block, and a scoped assertion, since the current acceptance criterion proves only the no-credential path.
NON-BLOCKING NOTE FOR P03, and it is a real architectural concern: every actor-trust clause now sits on the UNPRIVILEGED preflight job, so the privileged job's own condition carries no actor test at all. The auditor recommends duplicating the fork, draft, bot and association clauses onto the privileged job, and cites as evidence that this line is easy to break the fact that revision 6's own fixtures shipped the wrong gate predicate first. Bot exclusion is judged materially complete, because the bot logins the action's internal check wrongly trusts are all Bot-type actors and therefore a strict subset of what the five type checks reject; the residual is automation running under a User-type machine account, bounded only by the association clause.
Task (R18): revision 6 AS CORRECTED, sha256 c9e44318abbdf4131d0d8b575d318c442c8a89ff9fc8c573c8d92c5ecb441f2e, answering judgment finding JA-06. Both audits are stale against this hash and both must rerun, so this is materially a new round whatever it calls itself; the ledger identifies it by hash, which is unambiguous.
IT PROVED THE HOLE RATHER THAN REASONING ABOUT IT, which is the difference between this and a plausible correction. It executed the fixture's own credential-detecting step and observed that a pre-existing static credential yields the static shape, ZERO notice lines and a ZERO-BYTE step summary, against one notice and five hundred and forty-four bytes when no credential is found, and that each real job's gate is true on that shape. So both lanes go live, nothing is printed, and no act of acceptance is taken. That is the owner's design working exactly as instructed in a case the instruction did not anticipate.
Added: the finding's paragraph stating the case, who owns the residual by default in it, and why this revision exposes more downstream; one adopter-block entry in EACH fixture header, because the fixture is what gets copied; two acceptance criteria scoped to require the pre-existing-credential path rather than only the no-credential path; and a new unproven item carrying the auditor's architectural note that every actor-trust clause now sits on the unprivileged preflight, recommending the fork, draft, bot and association clauses be duplicated onto the privileged job, with revision 6's own wrong gate predicate as the evidence that the line is easy to break. Recorded, deliberately not implemented.
TWO FURTHER DEFECTS IT FOUND BEYOND THE AUDITOR'S LIST and disclosed rather than quietly fixing: the preflight description claimed unconditionally that it prints through both channels a reader sees, which is true only on the no-credential branch and is now scoped; and a line-range figure never matched its own log and was corrected, having moved again under this edit. Enumeration found seven locations and edited six, deliberately leaving one byte-identical and naming it by the qualification instead, disclosed in a new sweep paragraph.

Task (R69): Opus report collected, sha256 161fcbb42aadb8752c59d36a7ba337e1b69e717429110ad94c548ce649e0f91d, ten H3 fields with seven members, shape OK. It recommends a framework and runtime at named versions with one shared state type, native router composition, and a feature-gated binary with graceful shutdown inside a settings-driven timeout. For the owned parameter it proposes a single off-by-default feature gating the web module, the serve binary, the web tests and every downstream web layer, with the observability features nesting beneath it, and it MEASURED the cost: thirteen crates in the base tree, fifty-two with the feature on.
IT ANSWERED THE COUPLING QUESTION THE BRIEF ASKED FOR, which is the whole reason this item was admitted ahead of the gate: no CONFLICT, because R01's seam needs no change, verified specifically by establishing that the erased service reference satisfies the framework's state bound WITHOUT the service type deriving a clone trait. That is the exact failure mode that would have forced a published, owner-accepted decision to be reopened, and it was checked rather than assumed.
Two BASELINE-REVIEW lines, both carrying their evidence: one proposes dropping a lazy-configuration fallback because the framework's typed router makes a configuration-less application unrepresentable, and one demotes auto-reload to an optional recipe because one candidate is archived with a maintainer end-of-life notice, one declares no minimum version and so cannot clear that gate under the strict reading, and one is copyleft.
BOUNDARY QUESTION FOR THE SYNTHESIST AND AUDITORS, recorded rather than resolved: the worker reports executing a feature-off build and serving a handler over a real listener. The research-worker contract says do not scaffold a crate or write a server. The worker states it wrote only its own report file, so any probe lived outside the run, and the evidence it produced is exactly the evidence the coupling question needed. Whether that crosses the line is a judgement for the audits; it is flagged here so nobody has to discover it.
OWNER RULING, 2026-09-12, SUPERSEDES Q4 AND IS FAR WIDER THAN IT. The owner ruled: no licence check for any tool or library the template uses. The fixed `license = MIT OR Apache-2.0` applies ONLY to the source code in this repository directly, not to anything it includes or depends on. Q4 asked whether the gate reached separate-process developer tools; the answer given is that the gate does not reach dependencies at all.
SCOPE, established by reading rather than assumed: fitness gate 1, worded "license compatible with `MIT OR Apache-2.0`", appears in 85 of the 86 binding prompts, so this reinterprets an input every crate and bundle item was measured against. It is an owner INTERPRETATION of that gate, not a prompt edit; editing 85 prompts would invalidate the prompt hashes that published acceptance records cite, which is the defect R25 already carries for one prompt.
WHAT IT DOES NOT TOUCH: R46, per-file licence headers, is unaffected and stands. It governs the declaration of the repository's own source licence, which the owner's ruling explicitly preserves.
BLAST RADIUS, by item, established by searching every decision for a licence-based exclusion or qualification.
- R38, PUBLISHED: excluded `conventional_commits_linter` 0.17.1 explicitly "by gate 1" for being AGPL-3.0. That exclusion is now void. The recommendation is unlikely to move, because the same candidate was independently excluded on capability and evidence gates, but the stated reasoning is now wrong and a published decision should not carry a void ground.
- R42, PUBLISHED: chose the slower first-party install backend SPECIFICALLY to avoid this question, and stated in terms what changes if the owner ruled the other way: "only the recipe body changes to `cargo binstall --locked --no-confirm` - one line, no invocation line anywhere." Its recommendation changes under this ruling, and it told us in advance exactly how.
- R17, in revision: ANTICIPATED the ruling and already reasons this way, holding that the incumbent scanner's AGPL licence "attaches no obligation to `MIT OR Apache-2.0` template artifacts because the scanner is executed as a build tool and is never linked or redistributed." Confirmed rather than changed.
- R33, in revision: its three-readings structure and its strict branch, under which no snapshot stack existed, become moot and simplify to a single reading.
- R81, in revision: its conditional generator layer was conditional on this ruling and collapses to unconditional.
- R63 and R64, in revision, and R69's fresh Opus report, which demoted auto-reload partly because one candidate is AGPL-3.0: each carries a copyleft ground that is now void.
OWNER RULING Q7.B, 2026-09-12: the licence check is void ENTIRELY, including crates compiled into the shipped artifact. Gate 1 is struck from every item's evaluation. The owner was shown the distinction between separate-process tools, where nothing downstream inherits an obligation, and linked crates, whose terms travel into every downstream binary, and chose the wider rule knowingly. Recorded with that context so a future reader knows it was not an oversight.
AUDIT BATCH, 2026-09-12: six APPROVED and four REJECTED, all binding their current decisions. Approved: R26, R49, R64, R65, R81, and R18 on its seventh round, which is the JA-06 correction. Rejected: R17 on a false byte-preservation claim for named approved regions; R19 because one section is an unasserted live read rather than a pinned one; R60 because its revision-2 evidence scripts are not rerunnable once the revision is committed; R63 on a cited-log comparison that is not reproducible under its own stated rule. Every one of the four is a defect in the EVIDENCE rather than in a recommendation, which is where this programme's failures have concentrated all week.
THE COST OF THE RULING, and it is not small: two items that just passed their audits now carry reasoning the ruling voids, and three published items do too.
- R65, APPROVED MINUTES AGO: it EXCLUDED a candidate by gate 1. That candidate is now eligible and the recommendation may change, so the approval is on text that no longer reflects the rules. It needs a revision and a fresh audit pair.
- R81, APPROVED MINUTES AGO: its entire conditional structure, a documentation corpus decided unconditionally and a generator layer decided conditionally on this very ruling, collapses to unconditional. Same cost.
- R42, PUBLISHED: its recommendation changes. It chose the slower first-party backend specifically to avoid this question and stated the one-line change that follows if the owner ruled otherwise.
- R33, PUBLISHED: its three-readings structure and strict branch become moot.
- R38, PUBLISHED: carries an exclusion "by gate 1" that is now void, though the same candidate was independently excluded on capability and evidence grounds, so the conclusion stands with one ground removed.
R63 and R64 carry only passing licence notes, still factually true and no longer load-bearing; neither needs a revision for the ruling.
LICENCE REVIEW, GROUP 1 of 4 (R01, R04, R06, R07, R15, R16, R20). One AFFECTED, six MENTIONS ONLY, none needing its recommendation revisited.
R01 is AFFECTED and its recommendation does NOT move. Three crates were excluded with a licence clause among the grounds, the clause being that a permissive-only licence lacks the patent grant the owner's dual licence exists to provide, and one acceptance criterion requires the resolved pins to carry permissive licences. But every one of the three falls independently: one is at-risk with no push across two years of releases, one is at-risk and makes liberal use of unsafe code, and one is a dependency-injection container the chosen architecture rejects outright with zero reverse dependencies. So the repair is a reasoning edit at four named lines, not a reopened selection. The reviewer checked the independent grounds rather than reporting the exclusion and stopping, which is the distinction the brief asked for and the reason a coarse search would have produced a wrong answer here.
The other six are clean in substance: the licence appears as a gate row reading pass, as a retrieved figure, or as the word "inapplicable" where no crate is added at all. In each, the constraint that actually shaped the design is something else, most clearly in R20, where the chosen linter would fail the MINIMUM-VERSION gate as a dependency and is therefore consumed as a prebuilt binary, which is why the acquisition channel is part of the recommendation.
CONTROLLER RULING ON A JUDGEMENT CALL THE REVIEWER RAISED RATHER THAN DECIDED, and it was right to raise it. R16 records that the code-scanning engine's own USAGE TERMS restrict where analysis may run, a constraint inherited from the source repositories and owned by a separate ledger row's visibility gate. The reviewer classified it as a mention and asked to be overruled if the owner's ruling reaches usage terms. IT DOES NOT. The owner voided licence COMPATIBILITY checks on things the template depends on. A term restricting where a tool may lawfully run is a functional constraint on the tool's availability, not a question of whether its licence is compatible with ours, and voiding it would delete a real limit on where the scan works. R16 stands as classified.
LICENCE REVIEW, GROUP 2 of 4 (R21, R23, R25, R33, R34, R38). Two AFFECTED, four clean.
R33 confirmed AFFECTED, recommendation unchanged. Its three-readings structure and the strict branch under which it names no stack are pure conditionality on the voided gate. Under the plain reading the stack is exactly the three members already tabulated, so deleting the gate strikes a footnote, a whole section, one check, a clause, a re-verify trigger and an open question, and changes no selection. Its rejected alternative fell on downloads and reverse dependencies, not licence.
R38 AFFECTED, AND THE REVIEWER CORRECTED THE CONTROLLER ON THE POINT THAT MATTERS. I told the owner that R38's excluded candidate was independently excluded on capability and evidence grounds, so its conclusion would stand with one ground removed. That is WRONG, and the reviewer found why by reading the table's columns rather than its prose: the capability-and-evidence wording sits in the column recording the OPENAI PROVIDER REPORT'S position, while the decision's own Outcome column reads only "Excluded by gate 1". The decision never evaluated that candidate on capability and records no evidence for the provider's claim. So the sole stated ground is the licence, the exclusion is now unsupported, and the candidate re-enters the field UNEVALUATED.
Consequence, and it is larger than a reasoning edit: R38's pick is likely to survive, because the selected linter won on capability, prebuilt binaries for both required runners, declared minimum version and adoption, none of which referenced the excluded candidate. But R38 can no longer claim a complete capability filter over its field, so reopening it requires actually evaluating the re-entering candidate rather than deleting a sentence. That is real research, not bookkeeping.
CONTROLLER NOTE ON MY OWN ERROR: I reported the independent-grounds claim to the owner from a grep of prose, without checking which column the prose sat in. It is the same defect the programme keeps catching in decisions, committed by the controller while summarising for the owner, and it is the second time today my summary of an audit or decision was wrong in the owner's favour.
LICENCE REVIEW, GROUP 3 of 4 (R41, R42, R43, R45, R46, R47). One AFFECTED, five clean.
R42 AFFECTED and its RECOMMENDATION CHANGES, exactly as it predicted. Its own text says the stack degrades gracefully because only the recipe body changes, and the owner's ruling is broader than the condition it named, so it is satisfied a fortiori. The install layer's body becomes the fast prebuilt invocation; the reviewer confirmed that the only stated ground for demoting that backend was the licence finding, and that the owned parameter and every acceptance criterion are backend-agnostic and unchanged. Three passages go moot: the licence-clean prebuilt fallback, one unproven item, and a re-verify trigger watching for the backend to relicense. A second candidate was rejected partly on a copyleft licence but independently for imposing a whole package manager and an environment prefix, so it does not return.
The other five are clean, and one deserves recording because it is the distinction the owner drew. R46 decides whether the repository's own source files carry licence headers. The reviewer correctly classified it MENTIONS ONLY while noting that one line, holding that a permissive-only form fails the fixed expression outright, concerns THE REPOSITORY'S OWN DECLARED EXPRESSION rather than a third-party tool, which is precisely the case the owner's ruling preserves. A reviewer working from a word search would have flagged R46 as the most licence-laden item in the programme and been wrong.
RUNNING TOTAL after eighteen decisions: four affected. R01 and R33 need reasoning edits with no change of pick. R38 needs a candidate evaluated that its sole ground no longer excludes. R42 changes its recommendation.
LICENCE REVIEW, GROUP 4 of 4 (R67, R85, R18, R26, R49, R64, R65). Two AFFECTED, five clean, and neither affected item changes its recommendation.
R49 AFFECTED, reasoning only. It records that the fast install backend fails gate 1 as a dependency AND that it is out of scope regardless, because the manifest table it reads belongs to a different item. The scope ground is stated as sufficient on its own, in the decision's own word "regardless", so the exclusion survives the ruling intact. The pick is the toolchain's own manifest, called dominant because it is the only candidate that declares a build target at all, which no licence touches.
R65 AFFECTED, and it is the sharpest case in the whole review. It excludes the most-adopted candidate in its field, at six thousand six hundred and thirty-one reverse dependencies, SOLELY by gate 1 for carrying a weak copyleft licence, and the reviewer checked and found NO OTHER GROUND ANYWHERE INSIDE THE DECISION: that candidate appears in no gate row, no other exclusion list, and none of the three architecture comparisons. So as written the decision now rests a rejection of the field's leading crate on a gate that no longer exists.
The recommendation nonetheless survives, and the reason is in the raw report rather than the decision: that candidate is a RENDERER competing with the style-vocabulary member, and returning its type would export a renderer through the library's public surface, which is the same ground on which two other renderers were rejected; it also cannot fill the stream-adapter role the chosen architecture needs. So R65 must be reopened to state the surviving architectural reason, not to change its pick. A decision whose only stated ground for rejecting the most popular option has been struck cannot stand on silence.
R85, published, carries DEAD TEXT rather than a defect: a passage reasoning that a downstream consumer of the binary could no longer take one arm of the dual licence, and that a dependency-policy allowlist must therefore include the other. That is exactly the shipped-artifact reasoning the ruling voids. The recommendation and every exclusion stand on function and maintenance. Recorded for correction at its next reopening rather than reopened for prose.
CONTROLLER ERROR: R81 was omitted from all four review groups. Twenty-six of the twenty-seven approved decisions were assigned and one was not, and I did not notice until group 4 returned without it. A dedicated reviewer is now reading it.
LICENCE REVIEW, R81, the item the controller omitted from all four groups. AFFECTED, and it carries a STRUCTURAL GAP that none of the other twenty-six has.
Its recommendation changes: the documentation corpus was unconditional and the generator layer plus its hosted deployment were conditional on this exact ruling, with the decision stating in terms that absent the ruling the withholding branch ships. Under the owner's ruling the shipping branch becomes unconditional, which moves four ledger rows, adds the hosted URL, takes two command-runner recipes and flips one row's text while another's placement question goes live.
THE GAP, AND IT IS WHY THIS IS NOT A SWITCH FLIP: the decision chose the withholding branch USING THE LICENCE CONDITION AS A TIEBREAKER, EXPLICITLY INSTEAD OF MAKING A FIT JUDGEMENT. Its own words are that the tiebreaker is not a fit judgement but the licence condition. So no fit rationale for the shipping branch over the withholding one is stated ANYWHERE in the decision. Striking the gate does not reveal a hidden answer; it removes the only reason on record and leaves the question genuinely unanswered. The revision must make a judgement that was never made, which is research rather than editing. That puts R81 in the same class as R38, where a candidate re-enters unevaluated.
What survives and must NOT be stripped, all of it the repository's own source rather than a dependency: the fixed parameter line; the finding that none of the surveyed licences reaches the template's own source; and the rule forbidding the generator's theme directory from being committed, because that would place copyleft files INSIDE this repository, which is exactly the case the owner preserved. One further obligation survives for a different reason: an attribution notice owed if the built site emits certain assets is a distribution-notice duty on shipped CONTENT, not a tool-licence compatibility check, so the ruling does not void it.
Two candidates re-enter on licence and neither wins. One is a plugin the report itself calls a cheap future addition rather than a competitor. The other keeps a yanked placeholder registry entry and the wrong shape for the job, and loses to a first-party generator with documented source fidelity.
FINAL TALLY, twenty-seven approved decisions reviewed: seven affected. Recommendation changes at R42 and R81. Sole stated ground struck, requiring work rather than editing, at R38 and R81. Reasoning-only repairs at R01, R33, R49 and R65. Dead text at R85. Nineteen need nothing.
R42 REVISION 7, 2026-09-13, the FIRST decision revised under the owner's licence ruling. sha256 fd09fa1c682c049be39bfb841c0a57b1c6fff557ce5a16f0ba828dc4ceecabd1. The recipe body becomes `cargo binstall --locked --no-confirm <name>@<version>` at cargo-binstall 1.23.0, which is the change R42 itself predicted.
IT CONTRADICTED ITS OWN PREDICTION THREE TIMES RATHER THAN CONFIRMING IT, and that is why this revision is worth more than a search-and-replace. Revision 6 said "only the recipe body changes - one line, no invocation line anywhere." Revision 7 found: `--locked` binds only the `cargo install` fallback and not a prebuilt download; cargo-binstall must itself join the tool table, so the change is NOT literally one line; and prebuilt availability is per tool AND per target, with `cargo-shear@1.13.1` resolving an artifact while `taplo-cli@0.10.0` fell through to source. A synthesizer had every incentive to soften all three and did not.
It also removed the owner's email address from a script's User-Agent header, and disclosed that the same address sits in a frozen evidence log it deliberately did not touch. check_history passes, scripts/check-research-tree.sh prints OK, the owned parameter and all four `- assumes` lines are byte-identical, and acceptance criteria (A)-(E) are byte-identical.
CONTROLLER SWEEP, 2026-09-13: the owner's email address appears in 37 files across research/ and scripts/, three of them PUBLISHED under research/topics/. LEFT IN PLACE DELIBERATELY, and the owner should know rather than discover it: those files are frozen evidence bound by sha256 in published audit records, so editing them breaks the audit bindings that make the evidence checkable. The address is a business contact string of the kind crates.io's own User-Agent policy asks callers to supply, and it is NOT the git author address (that is a different address, already public in every commit). New scripts must use the repository URL instead; R42 revision 7 set that precedent and the standing agent rules now carry it.
R49 REVISION 3, 2026-09-13, sha256 85eddc5806c820229f9ad84b82fdc5d9c8e8098c9562105707a6584011794770. Reasoning repair only; the recommendation does not move.
IT DID THE CHECK THAT R38 TAUGHT US TO DO. The exclusion of the fast install backend rested on two grounds, one of them the now-void gate 1. Rather than assert the surviving scope ground, the agent established WHERE that ground lives: the `### Principles and implementation` H3, in the decision's OWN voice, tracing verbatim to the binding prompt's `## Out of scope` line. It further confirmed that NO raw report excluded the candidate on a licence ground - 28 matches across raw/*.md, none from codex, none mentioning GPL - so the gate-1 ground was synthesizer-added and removing it disturbs no engine position. The R38 failure mode, where the apparent independent ground turned out to sit in a provider-report column, is provably not present here.
Three locations carried a licence-as-gate claim and all three were edited; gate 1 is recorded as `inapplicable` rather than deleted. Eleven further licence mentions were left byte-identical with stated reasons, all of them the repository's OWN licence expression or measured registry metadata. The parameter-line diff is empty across all five lines. Two self-corrections were disclosed rather than made quietly: a sentence that overclaimed no check was re-run, and the mandated gate-1 string hard-wrapped across two lines so that a verbatim grep for it returned zero.
R69 COLLECTED IN FULL, 2026-09-13, and the delay was OURS, not Doxa's. All three Doxa providers finished on 2026-09-12 within minutes of submission. The operation sat uncollected for 24 hours.
ROOT CAUSE, and it is a trap worth recording: `doxa resume --json` WITHOUT `-A` short-circuits to a stale local checkpoint snapshot and reported all three providers as still `running` a full day after they had finished. A plain `resume --no-cancel-on-interrupt` polled the providers and saved all three reports within seconds. Before trusting either, read-only queries direct to the providers established the truth independently of Doxa: the OpenAI job completed with 193,235 total tokens and the Perplexity job COMPLETED in 341 seconds. `--no-cancel-on-interrupt` was used deliberately, because resume's default cancels the upstream paid jobs on interrupt.
The Codex report had been on disk since 2026-09-12T20:47Z with its operation record left at `submitting`, so the scan reported a FINISHED engine as in flight - the same reporting defect the Doxa diagnostic named at §8.1, in a different engine. Its receipt sha256 was verified against the file on disk before collection: match. Shape: 10 H3 fields, exactly the bundle template's headings in order.
doxa/combined.md is controller-built because Doxa 3.3.0's resume cannot honour the submission's `--combined` flag. THE BUILDER WAS PROVED RATHER THAN TRUSTED: it regenerates R49's committed combined.md byte-for-byte, sha256 9dbc22219bfcdc5b62657a7c03b400516a3914ca47fcbe6ea5190206db7654a3.
Per-provider shape: all three OK. The merged digest raw/doxa.normalized.md FAILS bundle validation, and it was DIAGNOSED rather than waved through: the digest is organised thematically under six H2 sections, and Gemini's members are emitted under `## Compatibility and Parameters` at merged-file line 1332 instead of under `## Framework and Tooling Members`; scripts/research_validation.py closes the `### Members` window at the next H1-H3 heading, orphaning 60 H5 member fields. That is a layout defect in a SUPPLEMENTARY artifact. The authoritative inputs are the three per-provider normalized files, which all pass, and raw/doxa.md, the verbatim concatenation, which is what the synthesizer reads.
Spend: USD 1.94 OpenAI, USD 0.90 Perplexity, Gemini read back as 854,283 input and 29,366 output tokens.
AUDIT INFRASTRUCTURE DEFECT FOUND AND FIXED, 2026-09-13, and it would have silently skipped audits. scripts codex-audit-rN.sh had been LOST from the scratchpad. KNOWN HAZARD, observed twice today and now understood: ten agents share ONE git index in this worktree. `git add` stages into that shared index, so whichever agent next runs `git commit` without paths commits everything staged, including other agents' files. It happened to the controller once, sweeping four R01 files into a ledger commit, and to the R38 agent once, whose four files landed inside a commit messaged for R19. NOTHING WAS LOST in either case and no agent amended, reset or reverted - each verified its committed bytes against its worktree instead. The fix in use: path-scoped `git commit -F - -- <paths>`, which commits only the named paths regardless of what else is staged.

R17 REVISION 4, 2026-09-13, sha256 81bf62f19c1ddabe96c762de4f142ef3a8ee4f344acdd51b7036712329c50554, PASS=59 FAIL=0.
THE AUDIT WAS RIGHT AND THE AGENT SAID SO. Revision 3 declared six regions preserved "as revision 2 wrote them"; THREE WERE NOT - the Part 2 recommendation, the ranked runner-up, and the section-08 validity-filter finding, each rewritten with allow-list wording. Conclusions unchanged, bytes changed, and the claim was about bytes. The repair is a CHECK, not a softer sentence: new section 13 reads revision 2 out of git at ed16bef1, asserts its digest so a wrong ref fails loudly, then compares fourteen anchor lines BOTH WAYS - eleven asserted byte-identical and the three the audit named asserted to DIFFER. An equality-only check could not have failed on the lines that changed.
THE FAIL-OPEN WAS REAL AND ONE LEVEL DEEPER THAN THE CONTROLLER DIAGNOSED. I reported that the rule covered three exit codes while the default shell exits on error. The allow-list was in fact complete on codes; the residual is that THE RULE NEVER SAID WHICH SHELL MAY CAPTURE THE CODE. The default `run:` shell `bash -e {0}` carries no `pipefail`, so a scanner piped into `tee` or `jq` hands the allow-list the PIPELINE'S last status, zero, and the job passes UNSCANNED. Section 12 executes it under the two shell command lines extracted from the pinned `github/docs` file and isolates the pipe as the mechanism: piped under `bash -e` the step exits 0 and the job passes, while the same under `shell: bash` and the same unpiped both fail the job. The rule now requires pipefail, forbids an unguarded pipe around the scanner, and DEMOTES the report-findings read from an alternative to an ADDITIONAL condition, because a scanner that never ran wrote no report and an absent or stale report reads as zero findings.
The missing capture was confirmed and closed by rerunning revision 3's committed script, which reproduced the cited log with three volatile lines moved and nothing else; the agent also checked the other same-shape ranges and found neither truncates. Six corrections beyond brief, including the owner's email in a crates.io User-Agent, a 255-versus-256 log-length contradiction, and two present-tense "is unchanged" sentences carrying the same defect class as F1.

THIRD SHARED-INDEX COLLISION TODAY, and the pattern is now fully characterised. R81's revision 3 landed inside commit 492d9b2, whose message describes R69. R81 revision 3 therefore has no commit message of its own, which is cosmetic and NOT worth rewriting history to fix. The mechanism: `git add` stages into a worktree-wide index, so a bare `git commit` by ANY agent commits every staged path. The fix, now used by the controller and independently reached by the R19, R49 and R60 agents, is the path-scoped form `git commit -F - -- <paths>`, which ignores the rest of the index entirely.

The synthesist hit this twice and mistook it for a real failure. An auditor seeing fetch-shaped failures must check for a concurrent run before reading them as a defect. This is pre-existing script behaviour, not new in revision 3, and with ten agents and a serial Codex runner in one worktree the collision is realistic rather than theoretical.

That is the correct way to carry a thin sample: state the size, match the wording to it, and name what does not rest on it.

J2 ANSWERED BY ESTABLISHING THE MECHANISM RATHER THAN GUESSING, and the answer is worse than the finding implied. PREBUILT ARTIFACTS VERIFY NOTHING ABSENT A CRATE-PUBLISHED KEY. Signature verification on a crate's own GitHub release is OPT-IN BY THE CRATE, not by the installer - the maintainers' own words are "This feature requires adding to the Cargo.toml metadata: no autodiscovery here!" - and with the default policy and no signing stanza the fetcher takes `SignatureVerifier::Noop`. NEITHER TOOL CHECK 15 EXERCISED OPTS IN: both are `signing: ABSENT` in their published manifests, and the recipe body run unchanged against cargo-shear@1.13.1 printed ZERO verified-signature lines.
THE POSITIVE CONTROL IS WHAT MAKES THAT ZERO EVIDENCE RATHER THAN ABSENCE: cargo-binstall 1.23.0 itself DOES publish a minisign key, and the same recipe printed exactly one `Verified signature` line for it. So the mechanism works and the check is not vacuous; the zero is the crate's missing key.
A SECOND LEG WAS ADDED BECAUSE THE AGENT CAUGHT ITSELF COMMITTING J2'S OWN DEFECT, and it disclosed this rather than quietly widening the check: its draft asserted "no checksum is compared either" on the strength of a source file leg 1 never read. Leg 2 therefore enumerates EVERY `DataVerifier` implementation across the whole tree and finds exactly three - a no-op, the minisign stream verifier, and a `Sha256Digest` constructed at ONE site only, checking a REGISTRY `.crate` against the index cksum. `binstalk-fetchers/src` contains no `cksum`, `sha256` or `digest` at all, so NEITHER prebuilt path compares a digest. The enumeration then found a third verifier the draft had not accounted for, and the text is scoped to it.
`--only-signed` WAS OBSERVED, NOT PREDICTED, and it does not do what its name suggests: run against an unsigned tool it does NOT error. Every prebuilt fetcher refuses and the run falls through to `cargo install --locked`, exit 0. It degrades a tool to revision 6's backend PER TOOL rather than failing closed.
Two further corrections beyond brief: QuickInstall was described as "always" verified when its own source shows the default policy LOGGING `Failed to download signature, skipping verification` and only `SignaturePolicy::Require` erroring; and the architecture table and disagreement 5 both claimed the pinned `name@version` spec "preserves" codex's provenance concern, which it does NOT, because codex's shape carried a SHA-256.
THE OWNER NOW HAS A REAL DECISION, and revision 8 does not pre-empt it. IT CORRECTED TWO FIGURES IN ITS OWN NEW TEXT BEFORE HANDING BACK, which is the behaviour this programme has been trying to produce all week: a count of 33 that did not sum to the log's 35, and a claim that whole-word matching means no ordinary word can match - FALSE, because `permissive` and `patent` are themselves ordinary English words. The surviving claim is the narrow one, that the printed list is the raw output of the stated command on the stated blob.
CONTROLLER DEFECT, 2026-09-14, caught within a minute and worth recording because it is the same genus as the failures being audited. Queueing R49's round-5 audit, I built the job row with `printf` and DOUBLE-quoted arguments containing backtick-quoted identifiers. zsh executed them as command substitutions: `mpl`, `permissive` and `patent` ran as commands, failed, and were replaced by EMPTY STRINGS. The brief therefore told the auditor that "all 35 dropped lines matched through the bare  alternative" and that a claim was false "because  and  are themselves ordinary words" - with the three crucial identifiers silently deleted. The corruption was invisible in the command I typed and visible only in the stored row.
The audit had already started against the corrupted brief. It was killed after roughly one minute, the row removed, the notes rebuilt from a quoted heredoc through Python so no shell interpretation touches them, and the presence of all three identifiers asserted before requeueing. STANDING RULE: audit-job notes are written from a quoted heredoc and inserted with Python, never through a double-quoted shell argument, because the payload routinely contains backticks, dollar signs and parentheses.
PROGRAMME-WIDE ENVIRONMENT FINDING, 2026-09-14, surfaced by R49's fourth rejection and larger than the item that produced it. EVERY AGENT'S `grep` IS NOT THE SAME PROGRAM AS EVERY AUDITOR'S `grep`.
- In an authoring shell - the controller's and every subagent's - `grep` is a SHELL FUNCTION defined in /Users/stevemorin/.claude/shell-snapshots/snapshot-zsh-*.sh, captured from the user's zsh profile. `type -a grep` lists the function FIRST and /usr/bin/grep second. That function routes to UGREP 7.8.4.
- `/usr/bin/grep`, the actual PATH binary, is `grep (BSD grep, GNU compatible) 2.6.0-FreeBSD`. `ugrep` is not on PATH under its own name.
- A Codex auditor's sandbox does not load that profile, so there `grep` IS BSD grep. The round-5 auditor reported exactly that and was right.
WHY THIS MATTERS FAR BEYOND R49: every evidence script in this corpus that types a bare `grep` runs UGREP while it is being written and BSD GREP when an auditor reruns it, when it is extracted from a `git archive`, or in CI. Those are different programs with different regex engines and different limits. The controller has already seen ugrep reject a pattern with "exceeds complexity limits" that BSD grep accepts. A script whose counts depend on which one ran is not reproducible, and the difference is INVISIBLE in the script's own text.
STANDING RULE, effective now: an evidence script that reports a count, a line list, or an absence must invoke `/usr/bin/grep` BY ABSOLUTE PATH, and must record the executable and its version beside the number. A count without its binary named is a weaker claim than it looks.
CONTROLLER ERROR, CORRECTED: I repeated R49's wording to the owner as "the PATH grep here is ugrep 7.8.4". That is false. The PATH grep is BSD grep; the SHELL FUNCTION is ugrep. I passed along a claim I had not checked, one message after recording a rule about not doing that.
R49 REJECTED A FOURTH TIME on that single wording, and the audit CONFIRMED everything else: the enumeration repair being true by construction, the proved filter rule, the six newly disclosed licence locations, and the provenance repair. The auditor also noted the claim altered no enumeration result, since both binaries return 40 for the old pattern. Revision 6 is directed to correct the wording precisely, name the binary by absolute path in the scripts, and give its judgement on switching the whole item's scripts to /usr/bin/grep.
THE AGENT'S OWN LOG CONTRADICTED ITS OWN PROSE, which is how the auditor caught it: review/evidence/licence-enumeration.log prints `grep(PATH): /usr/bin/grep` while the decision text said the PATH grep was ugrep. A cited log that disagrees with the sentence citing it is the cheapest defect in this programme to find and one of the most common.

THE OWNER WAS TOLD THE CHANGE WAS SMALLER THAN IT IS, AND THAT WAS MY ERROR. I put option (b) to the owner carrying R60's own prediction that "nothing else in this decision changes ... only the acceptance criteria AC2/AC3 are rewritten." THE PREDICTION IS FALSE, and the agent established why rather than absorbing it: `ArgAction::Version` returns immediately at clap_builder-4.6.6/src/parser/parser.rs:1322-1331, so the parse ENDS at `--version` and no format flag is ever read. Option (b) therefore needs `Command::disable_version_flag(true)` plus a template-declared global boolean - NOT clap's own documented recipe, which re-declares with `ArgAction::Version` and restores the short-circuit. Consequences: essential behaviors 1 and 2 rewritten, `ErrorKind::DisplayVersion` becomes UNREACHABLE so R67's routing shrinks by one kind, AC7 rewritten, and the F267 row rewritten. Revision 3's "only AC2/AC3" sentence is kept verbatim and corrected in place. The owner's choice is not disturbed by this; the cost of it was understated when I asked.
THE R66 COUPLING IS RESOLVED AS `related`, ON CHECKED EVIDENCE RATHER THAN CONVENIENCE. R66 owns NO registry parameter - its prompt's `- owns:` line is empty and PARAMETERS.md has no output-format row - so a `CONFLICT:` line, which names a consumed parameter whose value must change, would have INVENTED a registry row. CORRECTION TO THE PRECEDING GREP ENTRY, 2026-09-14, and the correction is mine. I wrote that "every evidence script in this corpus that types a bare `grep` runs UGREP while it is being written and BSD GREP when an auditor reruns it." THAT IS FALSE and it overstated the blast radius. R49's revision-6 agent established the real boundary, and the controller then verified it directly.
THE DISTINCTION IS TYPED COMMAND VERSUS SCRIPT, NOT MACHINE VERSUS MACHINE. Verified by probe: a typed `grep` in this session resolves to the shell function and prints `ugrep 7.8.4`; the identical bare `grep` inside `bash <script>` resolves to `/usr/bin/grep` and prints `grep (BSD grep, GNU compatible) 2.6.0-FreeBSD`. A bash script NEVER sees the zsh function, in any environment. EXISTING EVIDENCE SCRIPTS WERE THEREFORE NEVER AT RISK; the hazard is confined to searches TYPED AT A SHELL, which is exactly what the controller's licence sweeps and R49's failed census were.
THE REAL MECHANISM IS THE `--ignore-files` FLAG, and the agent DEMONSTRATED it rather than asserting it: the snapshot's function passes `-G --ignore-files --hidden -I --exclude-dir=.git`, so a RECURSIVE typed search HONOURS .gitignore and silently returns FEWER results. On a throwaway directory whose .gitignore ignores `ignored/`, /usr/bin/grep finds both files and the typed form finds only the visible one. Silently fewer results is precisely the failure mode this item was rejected for three times.
TWO LIMITS THE AGENT RECORDED SO THE FINDING IS NOT OVER-READ: explicitly named files, INCLUDING a shell-expanded glob, are read by both implementations, asserted in the script. So revision 3's failed census, `grep -rn 'binstall' raw/*.md | head -30`, had its glob expanded by the shell into explicit file arguments - ignore rules were NOT a second cause, and `head -30` remains its sole cause. And a script invoked as `bash <script>` already gets /usr/bin/grep everywhere, so this is not a defect in existing scripts.
THE REVISED STANDING RULE, narrower and therefore actually correct: NO COMPLETENESS CLAIM MAY REST ON A SEARCH TYPED AT A SHELL. New evidence scripts invoke /usr/bin/grep by absolute path and assert that binary's own version string beside any count. Existing reproduced scripts are left byte-identical - the agent deliberately left check-lockfile-inclusion.sh alone on the ground that perturbing an artifact three audits have reproduced, to fix a hazard it does not have, costs more than it buys. That is the right call.
A SEVENTH CHECK THAT COULD NOT FAIL, found by the agent in its own revision-5 script and dropped: its "PATH grep agrees" line ran a BARE grep inside a bash script, which is /usr/bin/grep - so it compared /usr/bin/grep against itself and could never disagree.

R42 REPUBLICATION PILOT REFUSED AT `publication apply`, 2026-09-16, AND THE REFUSAL IS REPO-WIDE: every publication since 2026-09-14 would have failed the same way. New run 2026-09-16T221533Z-42db28496e3b prepared and committed (36fccb4, run directory only); every step-0 precondition held by command (topic ca5a7310 equals the old receipt, check_history passes for real, both audits bind a2504aa7, OWNER row matches, no journal); the staged raw bytes reproduce the first publication exactly (doxa's staged file is the .normalized.md twin, 3d75b209, as `source_file` prefers); `build` succeeded with 39 changes and neither research/CLAUDE.md nor docs/port/PARAMETERS.md in them. `apply` then refused BEFORE the journal write: `staged structural validation failed: FAIL: docs/port/OWNER-REVIEW.md: table lacks column 'disposition'` and ~350 more including `owner review: R42 has 2 rows, expected exactly 1`.
ROOT CAUSE, read in scripts/check-research-tree-core.sh: `theader()` takes the header of the FIRST pipe table in the file and `ttsv()` reads EVERY pipe row in the whole file as that table's data. The Wave 0 acceptances table added to OWNER-REVIEW.md on 2026-09-14 (4177ae1) became the "owner review table" and every item then had two rows. `--require-owner-review` runs ONLY inside `_publish_unlocked`'s staged validation, so the plain `check-research-tree.sh` (exit 0 throughout) predicted nothing, and no publication had been attempted since R04 on 2026-09-05.
FIX, DOCUMENT-ONLY, NO CHECKER CHANGE: the acceptances section moved verbatim (46 lines, the re-bind/stale rule and every row) to docs/port/OWNER-ACCEPTANCES.md under a provenance header; OWNER-REVIEW.md keeps a pipe-free pointer and its single disposition table; the three 2026-09-15 rulings (W0-R42i, W0-R49n, W0-R17b) stay where they were under "Later owner rulings". Verified: `bash scripts/check-research-tree.sh --require-owner-review .` exit 0, plain checker exit 0, `scripts/test-check-research-tree.sh` 53 passed. The pilot's staged directory is reusable: all 39 expected_sha256 values are unmoved topic shas, so the same manifest re-applies without a rebuild.
RECIPE DEFECTS THE PILOT FOUND, folded into scratchpad/republication-recipe.md: step 0 must run `check-research-tree.sh --require-owner-review .` (the only pre-flight that exercises what `apply` validates); the packager stages EVERY review/evidence/*.log (32 here, not the 17 the first publication staged) and ignores .diff files; a pre-write refusal leaves no journal, so `publication recover` is wrong there and the prepared run is kept and committed because the scan adopts it immediately (`candidates[-1]`) whether or not it is published.
